ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
Henkel has always applied the latest standards for transparency in sustainability, ever since we published our
first Environmental Report in 1992. This year’s Sustainability Report marks the next step for this continuous
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) process because it applies the requirements of the European Union’s new Corporate Sustainability Reporting
Directive (CSRD) and its European Sustainability Reporting Standards (ESRS).
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) The ESRS aim to improve the availability, quality and comparability of sustainability information that companies
publish. In this first year, Henkel is voluntarily reporting in line with the ESRS except its requirement for
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
integrated reporting – because we still publish a separate Sustainability Report. We expect the ESRS to become
(ESRS S4)
mandatory under German law by 2025.
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
The transition to the ESRS involved challenges because it touches many parts of our business and involves
new methodologies, as well as new requirements for documentation and data. However, it has also provided
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
us with valuable insights, while opening up new governance processes and areas for collaboration. It is clear
that we are at the beginning of a journey and that further adjustments will be necessary in the next few years.
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 5
Beyond reporting, I would like to highlight the significant progress made in our key sustainability initiatives
FOREWORD
that provide long-term value for our business and continuously live up to our corporate purpose to be
SCOPE AND REFERENCE “Pioneers at heart for the good of generations.”
FRAMEWORK
I am particularly proud that we launched our SBTi-approved Net-Zero commitment for 2045 and set ambitious
GENERAL DISCLOSURES near-term climate targets for 2030. The targets are supported by a Climate Transition Plan and a new Nature
(ESRS 2)
Policy that confirm our commitment to taking action. In line with our previous climate targets, we made further
progress to reduce CO2 emissions in production by 64 percent per ton of product, compared to 2017.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
We also made significant progress on our sustainable packaging roadmap, reaching 25 percent of recycled
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
plastic use and 89 percent packaging recyclability overall. We also confirmed our wider ambition for circular
resource use by introducing a new Circularity Policy.
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
In our commitment to being a diverse, equitable and inclusive employer, we significantly stepped up our
ambition to reach gender parity in management. 42 percent of all management positions are now filled by
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) women. As inclusivity for all is important to us, we also introduced gender-neutral parental leave for our
employees worldwide. Henkel now offers parents up to eight weeks of fully paid parental leave, as well as
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR promoting equal parenting and childcare for all parents.
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Moving into 2025, we will continue to drive progress toward our targets and ambition, while further deepening
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
the integration of sustainability across our portfolio, our business processes and our supplier engagement
approach. We believe our commitment to sustainability makes a key contribution to ensuring the long-term
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) viability of our business by supporting the future of our planet and of communities worldwide.
Düsseldorf, February 7, 2025
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3)
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
(ESRS S4)
Sylvie Nicol
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
Executive Vice President Human Resources, Infrastructure and Sustainability

SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FOREWORD
SCOPE AND REFERENCE FRAMEWORK
FRAMEWORK
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
(ESRS 2)
Combined separate non-financial report with partial application of the European
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) Sustainability Reporting Standards (ESRS)
The Sustainability Report 2024 serves as the combined separate non-financial group report for the Henkel
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) Group and Henkel AG & Co. KGaA for fiscal 2024 within the meaning of Sections 315b and 315c, in con-
junction with 289b to 289e of the German Commercial Code (HGB), as well as the EU Taxonomy Regulation.
WATER AND MARINE
The Sustainability Report is made available to the public by publication on the Henkel website.
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
In preparing this report, we used the European Sustainability Reporting Standards (ESRS) as the framework
BIODIVERSITY AND
in accordance with Section 315c (3) in conjunction with Section 289d of the HGB. We followed their content
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
requirements, with the exception of the disclosure of the report as part of the group management report of
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR Henkel AG & Co. KGaA. The ESRS are being used as a framework for the first time due to the importance of
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) the ESRS as reporting standards for sustainability reporting adopted by the European Commission.
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) The information presented has been selected on the basis of the material sustainability topics identified in the
materiality assessment and the list of data points published by European Financial Reporting Advisory Group
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(EFRAG) (EFRAG Implementation Guidance – IG 3 List of ESRS data points). Data points that fall under voluntary
(ESRS S2)
reporting or phased transition provisions will not be considered by Henkel in the 2024 reporting year.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
Henkel reports in accordance with the general standard ESRS 2 (“General Disclosures”) and each of the ten
(ESRS S3)
topic-specific ESRS standards. For the reporting of topic-specific standards, we have adopted a consistent
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS chapter structure, with each section starting with an introduction that meets the disclosure requirement for
(ESRS S4) SBM-3 “Material impacts, risks, and opportunities and their interaction with strategy and business model.”
This approach is different from the chapter structure prescribed by the ESRS for the topic-specific standards
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
ESRS E1 and ESRS E4 because the presentation of transition plans (E1-1, E4-1) follows the reporting on SBM-3.

General basis for preparation (BP-1)
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
Basis for preparation (BP-1_5a)
This Sustainability Report was prepared on a consolidated basis.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
Scope of consolidation (BP-1_5b i)
WATER AND MARINE
The scope of consolidation for this Sustainability Report is the same as that of the consolidated financial
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
statements. Accordingly, references to Henkel’s own business activities in this Sustainability Report include
BIODIVERSITY AND all subsidiaries that have been fully consolidated in the consolidated financial statements based on the
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) concept of control. For further information, please refer to the consolidated financial statements, section
“Scope of consolidation.”
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Included subsidiaries (BP-1_5b ii)
None of the subsidiaries of Henkel AG & Co. KGaA based in the European Union is considered a public interest
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
entity. Accordingly, for the 2024 reporting, all subsidiaries of Henkel AG & Co. KGaA based in the European
Union are exempt from individual or consolidated sustainability reporting in accordance with section 289b
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) of the German Commercial Code (HGB), which transposes Article 19a (9) or 29a (8) of Directive 2013/34/EU
into German law.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) Coverage of the upstream and downstream value chain (BP-1_5c, AR 1)
This Sustainability Report covers Henkel’s own operations and those in its upstream and downstream value
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS chains. The materiality assessment of the impacts, risks and opportunities was carried out for the entire
(ESRS S4)
upstream and downstream value chain. The policies, actions and targets related to material impacts, risks and
opportunities generally cover the entire value chain. Furthermore, the option has been exercised to apply
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
the provisions for the phased-in disclosure requirements or data points, thereby deferring the reporting of
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION these mandatory disclosures to future years.
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 9
Option to omit information (BP-1_5d)
FOREWORD
According to ESRS 1_77, certain information related to intellectual property, know-how or the results of
SCOPE AND REFERENCE innovations may be omitted. This option was not applied by Henkel.
FRAMEWORK
Use of exemption (BP-1_5e)
GENERAL DISCLOSURES Henkel does not make use of the exemption allowing the omission of unfavorable information as provided
(ESRS 2) under section 289e of the German Commercial Code (HGB), which transposes Articles 19a (3) and 29a (3) of
Directive 2013/34/EU into German law.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
Disclosures in relation to specific circumstances (BP-2)
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
Definitions of time horizons (BP-2_9a)
The time horizons used for preparing the information in the Sustainability Report are in line with the recom-
WATER AND MARINE
mendation for time horizons as described in ESRS 1_6.4. Accordingly, “short-term” covers the reporting
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
period following the current reporting period (that is the year 2025 for the 2024 Sustainability Report),
BIODIVERSITY AND “medium-term” refers to the period from the end of the short-term reporting period up to the fifth reporting
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) period (that is from 2026 to the end of 2029 for the 2024 Sustainability Report) and “long-term” covers the
reporting period of more than five years up to the tenth year after the current reporting period (that is from
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
2030 to the end of 2034 for the 2024 Sustainability Report).
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Reasons for applying the definitions (BP-2_9b)
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
The limit of up to ten years following the current reporting period was chosen to ensure that the quality of
the forecasts and projections was sufficient. In addition, the long-term time frame matches the definition of
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) the long-term time horizon used in Henkel’s risk reporting.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES Value chain estimations (BP-2_10a, 10b, 10c, 10d)
(ESRS S3) The following table lists all key metrics published in this Sustainability Report for which data on the upstream
and/or downstream value chain have been estimated using sector averages or other proxies. The basis for
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS the creation and the resulting degree of accuracy is described for each metric listed. Similarly, actions to
(ESRS S4)
improve the accuracy of individual key figures in the future are explained where applicable.

Changes of sustainability information (BP-2_13, 13a, 13b, 13c)
FOREWORD
As this is the first separate Sustainability Report of Henkel prepared in accordance with the ESRS requirements
SCOPE AND REFERENCE to fulfill the reporting obligations under the still applicable Sections 289b to 289e and Sections 315b, 315c
FRAMEWORK of the German Commercial Code (HGB), there is no requirement to report on previous periods. Accordingly,
this Sustainability Report does not contain comparative information for prior periods.
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
(ESRS 2) Reporting errors in prior periods (BP-2_14a, 14b, 14c)
As this Sustainability Report is being prepared for the first time in accordance with the content requirements
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
of the ESRS, there is no requirement to report on reporting errors in prior periods.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) Disclosures stemming from other legislation or pronouncements (BP-2_15)
Disclosures have been included in the Sustainability Report due to the reporting requirements of the German
WATER AND MARINE
Commercial Code (HGB). This Sustainability Report also includes the disclosures required under Article 8 of
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
Regulation 2020/852 (EU Taxonomy Regulation) in the chapter “Environmental information.”
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) Incorporation of information by reference (BP-2_16)
The disclosure requirements of the ESRS have not been incorporated by reference. Further information to
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR which reference is made is not part of this Sustainability Report.

Governance
FOREWORD
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK The role of the administrative, management and supervisory bodies (GOV-1)
Number of executive and non-executive members (GOV-1_21a)
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
Henkel AG & Co. KGaA is a partnership limited by shares (KGaA). The sole personally liable partner of the
(ESRS 2)
Company is Henkel Management AG (MAG). Its Management Board is responsible for managing the
Company’s operations. The Management Board of MAG (Management Board) consists of five members.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
Four Management Board members are male and one is female.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
The Company’s Supervisory Board consists of sixteen members: eight shareholder representatives (four men
WATER AND MARINE and four women) and eight workers’ representatives (five men and three women). The Company’s articles
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) of association (Article 27) provide for the establishment of a Shareholders’ Committee. It currently has ten
members (nine men and one woman).
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) Representation of employees and other workers (GOV-1_21b)
Eight members of the Supervisory Board of the KGaA are workers’ representatives, including six employees
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
of the Company and its domestic subsidiaries and two representatives from trade unions. Other workers
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
who are not employees of the Company or its domestic subsidiaries are not represented on the Supervisory
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) Board.
Percentage by gender and other aspects of diversity (GOV-1_21d, GOV-1_21c, AR 5)
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2)
Management Board
Statutory gender quota for the Management Board
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) In a publicly listed company subject to the Co-Determination Act, if the Management Board has more than
three members, the Management Board must include at least one woman and one man (participation require-
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS ment pursuant to section 76 (3a) for the German Stock Corporation Act [AktG]).
(ESRS S4)
In accordance with this rule, the Management Board of Henkel Management AG, the sole personally liable
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
partner of Henkel AG & Co. KGaA as a publicly listed company subject to the Co-Determination Act, must
include at least one woman and one man if it consists of more than three members.

discussions within the Shareholders’ Committee and its Personnel Committee, to ensure a broad range of
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
knowledge, skills and professional experience (diversity) on the Management Board:

– Business understanding: Knowledge/experience in the industrial/consumer business and key markets,

knowledge/experience in the areas of marketing/sales and digitalization/e-commerce. Expertise in
research and development, production/technology and sustainable business practices, particularly in
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)

HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 26
 Internationality:
FOREWORD
The Company’s international activities in growth and mature markets should be adequately reflected in
SCOPE AND REFERENCE the composition of the Management Board. It is therefore intended that the Management Board should
FRAMEWORK include members of different nationalities or with an international background, such as significant profes-
sional experience abroad or management of foreign business activities.
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
(ESRS 2)
 Gender:
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) Both genders should be adequately represented on the Management Board. The Management Board
must include at least one woman and at least one man.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
 Seniority:
WATER AND MARINE
The composition of the Management Board should appropriately balance “change” and “continuity.” It is
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
intended that the Management Board should include members with varying levels of seniority. In general,
BIODIVERSITY AND Management Board members should not be older than 63 years.
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
We believe that the aforementioned objectives were fully achieved during the reporting year. The Management
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR Board, which includes one woman, collectively possesses the knowledge, skills and professional experience
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
required to properly perform its tasks. Several members also have international business experience in growth
and mature markets. No member of the Management Board exceeds the targeted maximum age.

Supervisory Board
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS Statutory gender quota for the Supervisory Board
(ESRS S2)
For Henkel AG & Co. KGaA, as a publicly listed company subject to the Co-Determination Act of 1976, at
least 30 percent of the Supervisory Board must consist of women and at least 30 percent of men (Section 96
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) paragraph 2 AktG).
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS Throughout the reporting year, shareholder and workers’ representatives consistently met the statutory
(ESRS S4) minimum requirements for gender representation. As of December 31, 2024, the Supervisory Board consisted
of nine men and seven women, including four men and four women on the shareholder representatives’ side
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
and five men and three women on the workers’ representatives’ side. This corresponds to approximately
56 percent men and 44 percent women. Until the re-election of shareholder representatives at the Annual

Sustainability-related performance in incentive schemes
(GOV-3_29a, 29b, 29c, 29d, AR 7)
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
In 2023, the remuneration system for members of the Management Board was adjusted and approved by
the Annual General Meeting of Henkel AG & Co. KGaA. The objective of this adjustment was to better support
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) our Company strategy and the sustainable development of the Company. The performance parameters for
the annual variable remuneration (Short Term Incentive, STI) are the financial targets achieved during the
BIODIVERSITY AND fiscal year (organic sales growth and adjusted earnings per preferred share, each weighted at 50 percent), as
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
well as the individual performance and goals (focus topics) of the Management Board members, which also
include ESG aspects. The performance parameters of the Long Term Incentive Plan (LTI Plan) – a virtual stock
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) plan with a three-year measurement period for target achievement and a subsequent one-year holding period
– include financial goals (adjusted return on capital employed, weighted at 60 percent, and relative total
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) shareholder return, weighted at 20 percent) and ESG targets (weighted at 20 percent).
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS The 2023 LTI tranche includes the following two ESG targets: 1. Reduction of CO2 emissions per metric ton of
(ESRS S2)
product at our production sites compared to the base year 2017 (weighting: 10 percent) and 2. Significant
progress toward Henkel’s ambition to achieve gender parity by 2025 (weighting: 10 percent). The 2024 LTI
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3)
tranche includes the following three ESG targets: 1. Reduction of CO2 emissions per metric ton of product at
our production sites compared to the base year 2017 (weighting: 6 percent), 2. Increasing the share of recycled
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS plastic for all packaging of our consumer products (weighting: 6 percent), and 3. Significant progress toward
(ESRS S4) the goal of achieving gender parity by 2025 (weighting: 8 percent).
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) For further details, please refer to the description of the remuneration system, which will also be included in
the remuneration report for fiscal year 2024.

Statement on due diligence (GOV-4)
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) Overview of due diligence process (GOV-4_32, AR 8, AR 9, AR 10)
The requirements for due diligence in the field of sustainability are considered when assessing material
WATER AND MARINE impacts. In human rights, for example, we take into account the risks for the rights holders. This risk analysis
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
is the basis for our human rights due diligence approach, which covers human rights and environmental
risks. At the same time, we closely track global and national developments on the understanding of terms,
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) on the assessment of risks, opportunities and impacts, and on the design of appropriate due diligence pro-
cedures. Our implementation of due diligence obligations is guided by the UN Guiding Principles on Business

Risk management and internal controls over sustainability reporting (GOV-5)
FOREWORD
Risk management and internal controls over sustainability reporting (GOV-5_36a, AR 11)
SCOPE AND REFERENCE Responsible and appropriate handling of risks and opportunities is a central part of Henkel’s corporate
FRAMEWORK governance. To identify and assess risks and opportunities at an early stage, we use coordinated risk manage-
ment and internal control systems that consider the nature and scope of Henkel’s business activities and
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
potential risks. Both systems incorporate sustainability matters.
(ESRS 2)
Henkel reports annually on sustainability – as early as 1992 with the first Environmental Report and then
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
continuously to the present Sustainability Report – and has extensive expertise in relevant topics such as
circularity, climate, nature and social issues. Regulations, rules and processes have been established to ensure
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
proper reporting. A significant portion of location-based data is systematically collected and verified using
WATER AND MARINE the principle of dual control. Global data is collected through centrally controlled processes, each verified for
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) plausibility by involving experts in the assessment. This ensures that the completeness and integrity of the
data and the accuracy of estimation results are taken into account in the data collection and reporting process,
BIODIVERSITY AND
including for the upstream and downstream value chain.
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
Risk assessment and prioritization (GOV-5_36b, AR 11)
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
We assess impacts, as well as risks and opportunities as part of our global sustainability management approach
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
including the materiality assessment for our Company along our value chain. As part of this process, we take
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) into account the different subject-specific understandings of risks. The validation of results and prioritization
of risks by experts will be increasingly formalized in the coming reporting years through the further develop-
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS ment of the internal control system for sustainability reporting.
(ESRS S2)
Main risks identified and mitigation strategies (GOV-5_36 c, AR 11)

VALUE CHAIN WORKERS visory bodies. Unforeseen significant changes are immediately reported to the CFO and the Compliance &

CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
and enables us to identify potential risks and business opportunities at an early stage. As part of the non-
(ESRS S4)

BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) risk mitigation that are described in this report. During this analysis of our own operations, our business
relationships, our products and our services, no material risks were identified that meet, or will meet, the
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION criteria of “having severe negative consequences” and “being very probable” according to the definitions
set out in Section 289c (2) and (3), as well as Section 315c of the German Commercial Code (HGB).

Sustainable business practices have been an integral part of our corporate culture for decades, and are also
(ESRS 2)

CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)

POLLUTION (ESRS E2) for our business and industrial customers, consumers and all other stakeholders. Our corporate strategy is
also embedded in the strategies of our business units. Additionally, our 2030+ Sustainability Ambition
WATER AND MARINE Framework addresses further topics that align with our high aspiration to create a transformational impact
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
for the good of generations. This aspiration also guides our approach to the individual current and future
topics. We continue to follow best practice on Environmental, Social and Governance (ESG) issues, integrate
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) independent opinions and expert advice and regularly incorporate these ideas into our strategic framework
and publications. Further information is available on the website at www.henkel.com/sustainability.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) Henkel’s business is organized in two business units: Adhesive Technologies and Consumer Brands. Our
Adhesive Technologies business unit offers technologies for adhesives, sealants and coatings – for industrial
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
applications and for consumers and craftsmen. Based on our technology portfolio, we provide customer-
centric solutions in our Mobility & Electronics, Packaging & Consumer Goods, and Craftsmen, Construction
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) & Professional business areas. The distribution channels include direct sales to our customers in industry and
trade, as well as brick-and-mortar retail, among others. In the Consumer Brands business unit, we focus on
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES the two global business areas Laundry & Home Care and Hair, and on the Other Consumer Businesses area
(ESRS S3)
with its operations in selective markets. Our portfolio features laundry detergents and household cleaners,
hair styling, hair colorants and hair care products for both the Consumer and Professional businesses, and
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
body care products. In all business areas, we aim to deliver high-performing innovations that create added
(ESRS S4)
value for our customers and consumers, as well as end-users. We distribute our products through brick-
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) and-mortar stores, hair salons and digital channels.

Strategic elements related to sustainability matters (SBM-1_40g; AR 12, AR 13),
FOREWORD
When it comes to global sustainability topics and developments, climate change is one of the greatest
SCOPE AND REFERENCE challenges humanity is facing today, requiring comprehensive action. It is also essential to protect and
FRAMEWORK regenerate our resources and life-support systems like forests, water and biodiversity, for present and future
generations. Additionally, we must address societal polarization driven by growing social inequality, which in
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
turn jeopardizes human rights and the basis of social coexistence. We acknowledge that companies like
(ESRS 2)
Henkel play a role in meeting these challenges and bringing about transformational change. Also in this way,
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) sustainability is closely integrated into our corporate strategy.
Our vision of sustainability and our corporate strategy incorporate the three dimensions of environment,
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
social and governance. We cover a broad range of topics, as reflected in the various topics presented in this
WATER AND MARINE Sustainability Report. In addition, we focus on key areas relevant to our business. Our business units align
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) their operations, brands and technologies with the specific challenges of their product portfolios in the
context of sustainable development, placing particular emphasis on these topics. Key focus areas include
BIODIVERSITY AND
climate, circular economy and safety, along with providing relevant information to customers and end-users.

RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR Incorporating interests of own workforce into the strategy and business model (SBM-2_12, AR 4, AR 5)
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) Our employees shape our Company through their dedication, knowledge and skills. They are crucial to our
long-term success. By fostering a strong corporate culture, shared values and collaboration as strong teams,
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
we drive our growth agenda. We build on our corporate purpose, “Pioneers at heart for the good of genera-
tions,” and our Leadership Commitments, which unite employees worldwide. We view cultural transformation
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) within the organization as an ongoing process, focusing on collaboration and empowerment while promoting
the development of our employees. Another key focus is our global initiatives on Diversity, Equity & Inclusion
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES (DEI). We are convinced that a diversified workforce, as well as an open and appreciative corporate culture,
(ESRS S3)
are important success factors in a globalized world.

We maintain continuous and intensive dialog with our workforce and their representatives to understand
FOREWORD
and fulfill their expectations. We conduct global employee surveys to uncover strengths and areas for im-
SCOPE AND REFERENCE provement in our corporate culture. For example, in 2022, we introduced the Henkel Pulse Check, a global
FRAMEWORK survey to ensure continuous employee feedback. Each month, at least 3,000 employees are randomly selected
to participate in the survey. The results reflect the organizational “pulse” and help identify strengths and
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
weaknesses, as well as derive targeted actions.
(ESRS 2)
Trusted cooperation with employee representative bodies is also a key element of our corporate culture. An
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
in-depth exchange of views and consultations with the employer takes place at the operational level, for ex-
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) ample with the local Works Councils, as well as across companies with the Works Council and the trade union.
Alternative mechanisms are in place to ensure dialog and exchange about working conditions in countries
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) where employee representation is not provided for or established, or for employees who are not covered by
a collective bargaining agreement. Some examples of this are town halls, employee committees or an open
BIODIVERSITY AND door policy. These serve as voluntary, informal instruments that facilitate a close exchange with the manage-
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
ment in each country.

Workers in the value chain (ESRS S2)
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Incorporating interests of value chain workers into the strategy and business model (SBM-2_9, AR 4, AR 5)
Henkel manages its value chain, which involves the procurement of materials and services for its operations,
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
in a way that aims to prevent potential negative impacts within supply chains.
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) As a matter of principle, we maintain intensive dialog with our suppliers to promote sustainable practices
and respect for human rights along the value chain.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) Our supplier base is one of our most important resources. It consists of a global workforce and is of great
importance, as intensive dialog and close collaboration with our suppliers are essential for implementing
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS sustainable business, process and production practices. With our comprehensive approach to responsible
(ESRS S4)
sourcing, we are already promoting sustainable practices and respect for human rights within our supply
chain. A key element of our strategic risk management and compliance approach is our six-step Responsible
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
Sourcing Process. This process is an integral part of our procurement activities and includes pre-checks and
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION risk assessments at the beginning of a collaboration with our suppliers, as well as in a recurring cycle of
evaluation, analysis and continuous improvement. We also work closely with stakeholders along the value
chain, from our suppliers to the Roundtable on Sustainable Palm Oil (RSPO), NGOs and smallholder farmers,
to promote sustainable practices and respect for human rights.

SCOPE AND REFERENCE (SBM-2_7, AR 3, AR 4)
FRAMEWORK Our commitment to respecting internationally recognized human rights throughout the entire value chain is
linked to protecting the environment and preserving and improving the livelihoods of communities along
GENERAL DISCLOSURES the value chain. We engage in dialog on these key topics with non-governmental organizations (NGOs) that
(ESRS 2)
represent the interests of communities. Collaborating with NGOs and thought leaders provides us with insights
into global challenges and enables us to respond to issues at an early stage. Building on previous projects,
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
such as the Social Impact Assessment, we strive to address stakeholder needs even more effectively and
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) ultimately strengthen our social influence across the entire value chain. In addition, our sustainability man-
agement approach includes understanding and reflecting the demands of various social stakeholders on the
WATER AND MARINE Company, such as neighbors, local communities and local authorities. Since our corporate activities are such
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
that we need to take into account our own business operations and the value chain, stakeholder dialog is
a key element of our due diligence efforts, helping us continuously refine our strategy and reporting.

FOREWORD
decision-making (SBM-3_48b)
SCOPE AND REFERENCE We assess the effects of our material impacts, risks and opportunities and strive to mitigate negative impacts,
FRAMEWORK enhance positive impacts, reduce risks and leverage opportunities. All material impacts, risks and opportunities,
including their current and expected effects, are strategically included as we understand sustainability as a
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
competitive edge. Accordingly, we also consider the connections to our business model, value chain, decision-
(ESRS 2)
making processes and our responses to these effects.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
How this is achieved with regard to the various impacts, risks and opportunities is explained in more detail in
the topic-specific chapters.

Current financial effects of key risks and opportunities (SBM-3_48d)
The current financial effects of the material risks and opportunities in 2024 are expected to impact the
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) operating result and, consequently, the earnings situation by up to 330 million euros. The financial position
and cash flows are affected to a similar extent. In determining this maximum value, certain aspects have
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS been estimated, such as the impact of specific costs, particularly CO2 prices, on the prices of raw materials
(ESRS S4)
and packaging materials. For further information, please refer to the section “Disclosures in relation to
specific circumstances“ (BP-2) in this chapter.

Short-, medium- and long-term anticipated financial effects of key risks and opportunities (SBM-3_48e i)
FOREWORD
The disclosure of anticipated financial effects of material risks and opportunities is subject to phase-in and is
SCOPE AND REFERENCE therefore not reported for the year 2024.
FRAMEWORK

WATER AND MARINE

RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
environment that is specific to Henkel.

opportunities to advance sustainability are constantly changing. We are committed to tackling these challenges

The evaluation of impacts was based on a gross assessment from Henkel’s perspective and followed a sector
FOREWORD
approach. The gross assessment approach assumes that no actions to prevent, minimize, mitigate or remediate
SCOPE AND REFERENCE impacts are taken into account (in accordance with “EFRAG IG1 – Materiality Assessment Implementation
FRAMEWORK Guidance, FAQ 23“). Accordingly, no company-specific mitigation actions were considered in the evaluation
of impacts, while industry and sector standards were treated as given. The gross approach evaluation of
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
impacts establishes a link to internationally recognized frameworks, including the OECD Guidelines for
(ESRS 2)
Multinational Enterprises and the United Nations Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights. This
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) approach is critical for Henkel in identifying and prioritizing impacts based on their inherent severity.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) Impacts were evaluated using a qualitative severity scoring system that considered all three attributes: scale,
scope and irremediability of impacts, as well as likelihood. Risks and opportunities were assessed based on
WATER AND MARINE financial magnitude and likelihood. For each of these criteria (severity, scale, scope, irremediability of impacts,
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
financial magnitude and likelihood), Henkel applied a standardized scale from 0 to 5. To facilitate the
evaluation process, additional guidance was provided by the central project team, including overviews and
BIODIVERSITY AND
detailed criteria with guiding questions. Each evaluation of impacts, risks and opportunities was supple-
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
mented by written justifications.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) For each topic-specific ESRS standard, the topic initiative (co-)leads were responsible for evaluating impacts.
For the evaluation of risks and opportunities, the topic initiatives provided qualitative aspects, while the
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) Sustainability Finance department, in collaboration with the business unit controlling teams, contributed
quantitative aspects regarding risks and coordinated the alignment between them. The Sustainability Portfolio
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
Assessments of the two business units were used for the evaluation of opportunities.
(ESRS S2)

To evaluate physical risks within the given time frames, Henkel used three potential future climate scenarios
FOREWORD
from the Shared Socioeconomic Pathways (SSP):
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK  SSP5-8.5 (3.2–5.4 degrees): Scenario where emissions continue to rise throughout the 21st century. This
path would lead to severe climate impacts, including extreme heatwaves, widespread habitat loss, rising
GENERAL DISCLOSURES sea levels and increased frequency of severe storms.
(ESRS 2)  SSP2-4.5 (1.7–3.2 degrees): Scenario where emissions peak around 2040 before declining. While less
harmful than SSP5-8.5, this pathway would still result in significant environmental changes, including
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
stressed ecosystems, more frequent extreme weather events and challenges to food and water security.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)  SSP1-2.6 (0.9–2.3 degrees): Scenario based on immediate, deep decarbonization efforts aligned with
goals similar to those of the Paris Agreement. If successful, this path would mitigate many severe climate
WATER AND MARINE impacts, help preserve ecosystems, limit sea level rise and reduce the frequency of extreme weather
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
events. Some impacts of climate change would still occur, but they would be easier to cope with.
BIODIVERSITY AND These SSP scenarios are used by the International Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) in its assessment reports.
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
Considering the SSP5-8.5 scenario enables Henkel to forecast the highest possible physical risks to the
Company based on scientific evidence.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Henkel evaluated the exposure of assets and operations to climate hazards under the three defined climate
scenarios, incorporating internal expert opinions on past events and known or anticipated risks. Global and
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
regional trends from climate research and literature were also considered. The assessment included the
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS likelihood, scale and duration of hazards for all components of the value chain: For the upstream value chain,
(ESRS S2) relevant feedstocks and supplier and logistics data were included in the assessment. For own operations,
Henkel’s sites were assessed. For the downstream value chain, an analysis was conducted to assess how climate
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
change may impact consumer and customer purchasing behavior, as well as markets and logistics.
(ESRS S3)

coordinates were considered, while other parts of the value chain were evaluated at a regional level. Henkel

FRAMEWORK leading to potential asset damage, risks to business continuity or increased costs can be identified. Risks in

RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
Climate-related transition risks and opportunities in own operations and along the upstream and
downstream value chain (IRO-1_20c, IRO-1_21, AR 12)
BIODIVERSITY AND
The World Energy Outlook (WEO) 2024, published by the International Energy Agency (IEA), presents a
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
detailed “Net-Zero by 2050” scenario, which outlines a specific, actionable pathway to reduce CO2 emissions
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR to net zero by the middle of the century. This scenario aligns with the goal of limiting the long-term global
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) average temperature increase to 1.5 degrees Celsius, the climate targets and the strategic focus on achieving
a net-zero transition. Henkel has therefore assessed transition risks and opportunities based on the assumption
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
of a net-zero scenario by 2050, taking into account the resulting changes in political and technological as-
sumptions, macroeconomic trends, energy consumption and the energy mix.
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2)
For the climate-related transition risks and opportunities, all transition events in the areas of politics and law,
technology, market and reputation from the classifications of the Task Force on Climate-related Financial
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) Disclosures (TCFD), including all internally known risks and opportunities, were assessed. A key area of concern
for Henkel is the potential financial impact of increasing CO2 prices, for example, through taxation or emissions
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS trading systems. The increase in CO2 prices can lead to higher procurement costs and product prices for
(ESRS S4)
Henkel. Henkel assesses the potential direct and indirect impacts of rising CO2 prices by assigning a “CO2
shadow price” to its GHG emissions. For the year 2030, Henkel applied regionally differentiated assumptions
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
regarding CO2 prices, as described in the WEO. To maintain a conservative approach, Henkel based its
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION calculations on constant GHG emissions across its value chain and own operations, using 2023 as the baseline.
This assumption allows for a conservative estimation. However, it is important to note that the estimation does
not reflect the expected reductions outlined in Henkel’s climate strategy and Climate Transition Plan (CTP).
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 98
Given that emissions in Henkel’s upstream and downstream value chains account for the majority of the
FOREWORD
Company’s total emissions, the financial impact of a CO2 shadow price are most significant for these areas.
SCOPE AND REFERENCE It was determined that the cost impact in the upstream value chain (that is for raw materials and packaging)
FRAMEWORK could be financially material under the assumptions (gross assessment without mitigating actions).
GENERAL DISCLOSURES In downstream operations, increased CO2 prices and additional political or regulatory measures could lead
(ESRS 2)
to higher production and transportation costs, potentially affecting customer and consumer behavior over
the long term. This could negatively impact Henkel’s competitiveness and lead to revenue losses. Such devel-
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
opments could also result in downstream risks becoming a material risk in the long-term.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
Transition risks related to Henkel’s own operations have also been assessed. However, they have not been
identified as financially material.
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
To mitigate the identified gross risks, Henkel’s Climate Transition Plan (CTP) serves as a strategic element.
It defines the Company’s net-zero targets, outlines its emission reduction activities to achieve these goals
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) and establishes its governance and delivery mechanisms for implementing the net-zero transformation. It
should be noted that certain risks addressed in other standards, such as risks associated with replacing petro-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR chemical raw materials with renewable resources in the topic-specific standard ESRS E5 (“Resource Use and
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Circular Economy”), are also related to climate-relevant transition risks.

Description of the processes to identify and assess material pollution-related impacts, risks and
opportunities (E2 ESRS 2 IRO-1)
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
Review of sites and business activities (IRO-1_11a, AR 1)
Henkel’s materiality assessment includes the evaluation of impacts, risks and opportunities. The impacts
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) were assessed based on a sector perspective.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES The analysis of pollution focused on Henkel’s own operations, potential pollution within upstream supply
(ESRS S3) chains and pollution caused by Henkel products. The impact assessment used an environmental risk analysis
method. It assumes no material impacts if the expected environmental concentration remains below effect
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
thresholds. The evaluation of risks and opportunities is based on estimates of the financial extent of business
(ESRS S4)
losses or gains and on the likelihood of their occurrence. Examples of assumed reasons for business losses
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) include increased expenditures for reducing pollution at Henkel sites, higher procurement costs or decreased
revenues due to regulatory restrictions or reduced product acceptance among customers.

Consultations, particularly with affected communities (IRO-1_11b)

WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) as in adhesives, sealants and coatings, Henkel generally has no material impact on threatened species in
ecosystems with vulnerable biodiversity. Soil sealing, habitat fragmentation or construction in sensitive eco-
BIODIVERSITY AND systems are not among Henkel’s business activities. The potential impacts of individual sites with biodiversity
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
relevance are described in the chapter “Biodiversity and Ecosystems” (ESRS E4). According to the directives
and frameworks or equivalent national or international standards listed below, no specific mitigation
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR

 Directive 2009/147/EC of the European Parliament and of the Council on the conservation of wild birds:
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
This Directive addresses the protection of endangered bird species and the governmental actions of
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS member states to protect these habitats, such as prohibiting hunting or creating protected areas. Henkel’s
(ESRS S2) business activities as a company for consumer goods (laundry and home care, hair and body care) as
well as adhesives, sealants and coatings, do not involve trade in endangered bird species. The Company
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES

HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 116
Determining material information, including the use of thresholds (IRO-2_59)
FOREWORD
To determine the materiality of impacts and financial materiality (that is risks and opportunities), Henkel
SCOPE AND REFERENCE followed a heatmap approach. Two matrices were used, one for the impacts and one for risks and opportunities.
FRAMEWORK
To determine the materiality, the impacts were classified in a matrix on a six-point scale (scales 0–5) according
GENERAL DISCLOSURES to their assessment in the two dimensions “severity” and “likelihood.” The actual impacts have a likelihood
(ESRS 2) value of “5,” while potential impacts are classified with a maximum value of “4.” All impacts at Henkel that
are assessed at least “severe” (severity scale: 3 to 5) and at least “more likely than not” (likelihood scale: 3 to 5)
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
are classified as material. The purpose of setting these thresholds is to focus on the severe impacts that are
combined with a medium and high likelihood. In addition, impacts with a higher degree of severity (sever-
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
ity scale: 4 and 5) and a lower likelihood (likelihood scale: 2) were also classified as material. Similarly, im-
WATER AND MARINE pacts with the highest severity level (severity scale: 5), which are unlikely to occur (likelihood scale: 1), were
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) classified as material.
BIODIVERSITY AND Regarding the determination of financial materiality for risks and opportunities, Henkel also uses a matrix
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
where the two dimensions of “financial magnitude” – measured as the annual impact on operating profit –
and “likelihood” were classified on a six-point scale. Actual risks or opportunities have a likelihood value of
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) “5,” while potential risks or opportunities are classified with a maximum value of “4.” All risks and opportunities
that Henkel assesses as having a financial magnitude of at least a high annual EBIT impact (scale of financial
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) magnitude: 3 to 5) and as at least “more likely than not” (scale of likelihood: 3 to 5) were classified as material.
In addition, risks and opportunities with a higher financial magnitude (scale of financial magnitude: 4 and 5)
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
and lower likelihood (scale of likelihood: 2) were also classified as material. Substantial risks respectively
(ESRS S2)
substantial opportunities (scale of financial magnitude: 5) that are unlikely to materialize (scale of likelihood: 1)
were likewise classified as material.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3)
The determination of the assessment criteria and thresholds was the result of an iterative coordination process
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS as part of the materiality assessment and with the involvement of the relevant stakeholders (in accordance
(ESRS S4) with IRO-1_53d). Both external sources such as the document “EFRAG IG1 – Materiality Assessment Imple-
mentation Guidance” and internal references such as the risk management requirements were used as the
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
basis for the determination.

ENVIRONMENTAL
FOREWORD
SCOPE AND REFERENCE INFORMATION
FRAMEWORK
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
(ESRS 2)
Disclosures pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) (EU) 2020/852 (Taxonomy Regulation)
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
The EU Taxonomy is a central instrument of the European Union’s “Green Deal.” The aim of this EU-wide
classification system for economic activities is to promote investment in environmentally sustainable
WATER AND MARINE
economic activities.
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
According to the Regulation (EU) 2020/852, an economic activity is considered environmentally sustainable
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) if it substantially contributes to achieving one or more of the defined environmental objectives. At the same
time, an economic activity must not significantly harm other environmental objectives. The six environmental
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR objectives of the EU Taxonomy Regulation are: climate change mitigation (CCM); climate change adaptation
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
(CCA); the sustainable use and protection of water and marine resources (WTR); the transition to a circular
economy (CE); pollution prevention and control (PPC); and the protection and restoration of biodiversity and
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
ecosystems (BIO). According to the EU Taxonomy, the economic activity must also be carried out in compliance
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS with the minimum safeguards and meet the technical screening criteria defined by the EU Commission by
(ESRS S2) means of delegated acts. The technical screening criteria specify the performance requirements for a specific
economic activity, determining to which extent the activity makes a substantial contribution to achieving a
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
particular environmental objective and does not significantly harm (DNSH) the other objectives.
(ESRS S3)
Economic activities that fall within the scope of the EU Taxonomy are referred to as “Taxonomy-eligible.”
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
Economic activities that additionally meet all of the aforementioned criteria are categorized as “Taxonomy-
(ESRS S4)
aligned.” For all activities (“Taxonomy-eligible” and “Taxonomy-aligned”) key performance indicators and
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) qualitative information is required by the EU Taxonomy.
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION Accounting policy
The EU Taxonomy comprises three key performance indicators (KPI): turnover, operating expenditures
(OpEx) and capital expenditures (CapEx). The definition and the subsequent determination of these KPIs
under the EU Taxonomy are based on the International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS).
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 118
Turnover is therefore determined in accordance with IFRS15 “Revenue from Contracts with Customers.” For
FOREWORD
further information on turnover, please refer to the corresponding item “Sales” in the consolidated income
SCOPE AND REFERENCE statement in the Annual Report 2024 on page 211. For further information on the principles of revenue
FRAMEWORK recognition, please refer to Note 24 (pages 321 and 322) of the Annual Report 2024.
GENERAL DISCLOSURES According to the EU Taxonomy, the denominator of operating expenditure includes direct non-capitalized
(ESRS 2)
costs that relate to research and development, building renovation measures, short-term leases, maintenance
and repair, and any other direct expenditures relating to the day-to-day servicing of assets of property, plant
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
and equipment. This includes research and development expenses recognized as an expense in the income
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) statement during the reporting period in accordance with IAS 38 (see page 323 of our Annual Report 2024).
Lease expenses were determined in accordance with IFRS 16 and include expenses for short-term leases and
WATER AND MARINE leases of low-value assets (see pages 252 and 253 of our Annual Report 2024). Maintenance and repair costs
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
and costs for building renovation measures are calculated and allocated appropriately.
BIODIVERSITY AND The share of operating expenditure (OpEx-KPI) associated with economic activities that are categorized as
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
Taxonomy-aligned is determined as follows: Proportion of total operating expenditure that is Taxonomy-
aligned (numerator) divided by total operating expenditure as defined in Annex I of the Delegated Regulation
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) on reporting obligations (denominator).
The share of capital expenditures (CapEx-KPI) associated with economic activities that are categorized as
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
Taxonomy-aligned is calculated as follows: Proportion of total capital expenditure that is Taxonomy-aligned
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS (numerator) divided by total capital expenditure in accordance with Annex I of the Delegated Regulation on
(ESRS S2) reporting requirements (denominator).
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES The determination of the denominator of capital expenditure follows IAS 16 “Property, Plant and Equipment”
(ESRS S3)
(IAS 16.73(e)(i) and (iii)), IAS 38 “Intangible Assets” (IAS 38.118(e)(i)) and IFRS 16 “Leases” (IFRS 16.53(h)).
CapEx for 2024 are calculated based on the aggregate of the corresponding lines “Acquisitions,” “Additions
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
(ESRS S4) to existing business” and “Additions of right-of-use assets” under property, plant and equipment in the
Annual Report 2024 on page 249 and “Acquisitions” and “Additions” under intangible assets, page 243 in
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) the Annual Report 2024. Goodwill is not included in CapEx for the purposes of EU Taxonomy reporting. For
further information on property, plant and equipment, intangible assets and leases, please refer to pages
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
241 to 252 of the Annual Report 2024.

POLLUTION (ESRS E2) Taxonomy-aligned capital expenditure
In order to demonstrate Taxonomy-alignment of the aforementioned additions to intangible assets and
WATER AND MARINE
property, plant and equipment, we conducted a comprehensive analysis of the technical screening criteria
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)

BIODIVERSITY AND screening criteria continued to pose challenges for both Henkel and its suppliers. Suppliers were either unable
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) to provide the required information at all or not in a timely manner. Furthermore, the criteria for the Taxonomy-
alignment of the activities for most investment projects and leasing contracts were not met or could not be
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR

ECONOMY (ESRS E5)

OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)

HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 130
Climate change (ESRS E1)
FOREWORD
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK Strategy
Material impacts, risks and opportunities and their interaction with strategy and business model
GENERAL DISCLOSURES (ESRS 2, SBM-3_48b)
(ESRS 2)
Climate change is one of the major global challenges of our time. Henkel considers climate change to be a
factor that causes various risks, but that also offers numerous opportunities at the same time. Climate
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
change mitigation and energy are material topics for Henkel. Negative impacts on climate change are linked
to Henkel’s business model as a company for consumer goods (laundry and home care, hair and body care)
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
and adhesives, sealants and coatings. Energy is used and greenhouse gases are omitted in the Company’s
WATER AND MARINE production processes, and in the upstream and downstream value chain. Moreover, Henkel identified CO2
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
taxation in its upstream and downstream value chain as a potential material risk in regard to financial effects.
However, there are also potential material economic opportunities in marketing products with a reduced
BIODIVERSITY AND
carbon footprint. For adhesives, sealants and coatings, replacement of existing technologies with energy-
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
efficient and carbon-friendly alternatives is an opportunity and has positive impacts on many sectors that
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR make a material contribution to climate change.
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Henkel assumes responsibility for reducing its impacts on climate change. For this reason, the Company is
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) aligning its strategy with the 1.5-degree Celsius target of the Paris Climate Agreement and mitigating climate
change. We consider sustainability in our strategy to be a competitive advantage.
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) Henkel has set a target to achieve net-zero greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in its entire value chain by
2045. Henkel’s transition plan is a key component of the climate strategy, which provides for an end-to-end
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
organizational and cultural shift toward net-zero. Topics such as energy use, energy efficiency, renewable
(ESRS S3)
energies, ecodesign of products and packaging, use of low-carbon materials and low-carbon logistics are at
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS the forefront of actions adopted for implementation.
(ESRS S4)
The ambitions and targets for climate change mitigation are a key element in Henkel’s strategy and business
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) model with regard to its own activities, and within the upstream and downstream value chain. The double
materiality assessment carried out did not identify any material dependencies with regard to climate change.
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 131
Climate-related risk types (ESRS 2, SBM-3_18)
FOREWORD
Henkel determined two potential material climate-related risks in the materiality assessment. Both risks can
SCOPE AND REFERENCE be categorized as climate-related transition risks and relate to the sub-topic “climate change mitigation.”
FRAMEWORK The first potential risk lies in the upstream value chain and relates to political and legal actions for reducing
carbon emissions such as imposing carbon taxes. These can increase production and transportation costs in
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
the medium term, which leads to higher raw material prices and possible raw material shortages. The second
(ESRS 2)
potential risk lies in the downstream value chain, and also relates to political and legal actions for reducing
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) carbon emissions such as imposing carbon taxes. In addition to the stated risks, the usage behavior of con-
sumers and customers can change, which can lead to revenue losses.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
Scope of the resilience analysis (ESRS 2, SBM-3_19a, AR 16)
WATER AND MARINE The resilience analysis considered physical risks and transition risks for Henkel’s business activities and for
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) the value chain. The definition of the EU Taxonomy for sustainable activities is used for physical climate risks
(this includes 28 climate risks including wildfires, storms and heat waves). The assessment of the physical
BIODIVERSITY AND
risks in the upstream value chain assessed climate-related risks for the relevant raw materials, suppliers and
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
logistical services. For Henkel’s own operations, the climate-related risks for its own production sites were
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR assessed. The assessment of impacts of climate-related risks on the downstream value chain looked at the
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) impacts on customers and consumers, on Henkel’s markets and logistical services. Transition risks were
defined as risks relating to the transition to an economy with reduced GHG emissions and are based on clas-
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
sifications of the Task Force on Climate-related Financial Disclosures (TCFD). For the assessment of climate-
related transition risks and opportunities, potential regulatory changes in politics and law, technological
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) changes and changes in customer and consumer behavior were examined. For the resilience analysis, the
transition risks were assessed qualitatively at the strategic level and based on expert predictions, while the
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES potential impacts of CO2 prices were assessed quantitatively.
(ESRS S3)
Timing and implementation of the resilience analysis (ESRS 2, SBM-3_19b, AR 7)
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS The resilience analysis was carried out in mid-2024 by a task force assigned for the purpose in order to assess
(ESRS S4)
the resilience of Henkel’s strategy and business activities in view of climate change. The analysis was based
on the knowledge of internal and external experts and stakeholders, geographical models and relevant liter-
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
ature. Although the climate scenarios and analysis as such look ahead to 2050 and beyond, the time hori-
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION zons used for the risk assessments were aligned with the time horizons defined for the materiality assess-
ment. These are defined in the section “Definitions of time horizons” (BP-2_9a) in the chapter “General dis-
closures” (ESRS 2). Regulatory, economic and societal changes are likely to impact the surrounding macro-
economic trends, energy consumption and energy mix, as well as assumptions about the use of technology
in dependence on the climate scenarios considered. For further details, please refer to the section “Descrip-
tion of the processes to identify and assess material climate-related impacts, risks and opportunities”
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 132
(E1 ESRS IRO-1) in the chapter “General disclosures” (ESRS 2). Consideration of the “Net Zero by 2050”
FOREWORD
scenario is in line with Henkel’s net-zero strategy and the associated targets relating to the reduction of
SCOPE AND REFERENCE GHG emissions and the actions defined in the transition plan for climate change mitigation.
FRAMEWORK
Results of the resilience analysis (ESRS 2 SBM-3_19c, AR 8, AR 13)
GENERAL DISCLOSURES As a forward-looking analysis was carried out, uncertainties cannot be excluded. The resilience analysis is a
(ESRS 2) comprehensive analysis of climate risks and the possible impacts on Henkel. It is based on current method-
ologies, the latest climate projections and available scientific literature. For further details, such as relevant
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
studies or sources, please refer to the section “Description of the processes to identify and assess material
climate-related impacts, risks and opportunities” (E1 ESRS 2 IRO-1) in the chapter “General disclosures” (ESRS 2).
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
The resulting material risks and opportunities are linked to climate change and relate to the upstream and
WATER AND MARINE downstream value chain. It can be assumed that changes to legal requirements will have a financial impact
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) on the Company, that is through increasing carbon taxes or market restrictions. However, these changes
also offer opportunities based on changing customer and consumer demand and the marketing of low-
BIODIVERSITY AND
carbon and climate-neutral products. The resilience analysis shows that Henkel must consider the anticipated
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
changes to the surrounding macroeconomic trends and prepare itself for changes to energy use and the
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR energy mix, and for the use of appropriate technology because of climate change. Subsequently, climate-
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) related transition risks and opportunities are an inherent part of Henkel’s corporate strategy for reducing
emissions. The strategic focus shows that the Company is dedicated to adapting itself to the anticipated
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
changes in order to minimize short-term, medium-term and long-term climate-related risks and to make use
of opportunities.

Transition plan for climate change mitigation (E1-1)
FOREWORD
Transition plan for climate change mitigation (E1-1_14, AR 1, E1-1_17)
SCOPE AND REFERENCE Henkel is committed to the Paris Climate Agreement and aims to comply with the nationally determined
FRAMEWORK contributions (NDCs) of the countries in which it operates in order to limit global warming to 1.5 degrees
Celsius. Henkel considers the transition to a low-carbon economy to be a decisive prerequisite for securing
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
its future competitiveness and reducing negative impacts for society and its stakeholders, while also creating
(ESRS 2)
added value. Henkel’s transition plan for climate change mitigation serves as a strategic element of our climate
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) change mitigation approach and defines how the strategy and the business model should be adapted to
ensure that they are compatible with limiting global warming to 1.5 degrees Celsius.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
To support the transition to a low-carbon economy and limit global warming to 1.5 degrees Celsius, Henkel
WATER AND MARINE is integrating climate change mitigation into its central corporate strategy and business activities. On the
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) one hand, developing a net-zero roadmap by determining science-based targets to achieve net-zero emissions
is of critical importance. For further information, please refer to the following section “Compatibility of the
BIODIVERSITY AND
targets with the 1.5-degree Celsius target of the Paris Climate Agreement” (E1-1_16a). On the other hand,
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
the implementation of the net-zero transition plan involves adaptations to the product portfolio and the intro-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR duction of new technologies and solutions within the Company itself or in the upstream and downstream
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) value chain. For further information, please refer to the section “Decarbonization levers and key actions
planned” (E1-1_16b) in this chapter.
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
Compatibility of the targets with the 1.5-degree Celsius target of the Paris Climate Agreement
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS (E1-1_16a, AR 2, AR 26, AR 27)
(ESRS S2)
Henkel’s climate targets for 2030 and its net-zero target were defined according to guidelines, criteria and
recommendations of the Corporate Net-Zero Standard of and officially validated by the Science Based Tar-
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
gets initiative (SBTi). Henkel’s science-based targets thereby comply with the requirements needed to limit
(ESRS S3)
the global temperature increase to 1.5 degrees Celsius. In line with the SBTi Corporate Net-Zero Standard,
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS the targets for reducing emissions cover the seven greenhouse gases of the Kyoto Protocol and all the rele-
(ESRS S4) vant categories of the Scopes that are defined by the Corporate Standard of the Greenhouse Gas (GHG)
Protocol. The Scope categories 3.8, 3.10, 3.13 and 3.14 are covered by the target but are not relevant in the
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
base and reporting year due to the Henkel business model. Indirect emissions from the use phase are an
exception here because these emissions cannot be part of the prescribed emission scope of the target accord-
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
ing to the SBTi. Henkel’s targets are absolute emissions reduction targets. As there is currently no finalized
version of the sector-specific guidelines for the chemicals sector, the guidelines of the cross-sectoral path-
way of the SBTi were used.

FOREWORD
1. Henkel commits to reduce absolute Scope 1 and Scope 2 GHG emissions by 42 percent by 2030 from a

FRAMEWORK
2. Henkel commits to reduce absolute Scope 3 GHG emissions by 30 percent by 2030 from a 2021 base
GENERAL DISCLOSURES year.
(ESRS 2)
SBTi net-zero climate target: According to the SBTi definition, net-zero is defined as achieving an absolute
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) reduction of at least 90 percent of emissions across the entire value chain, i.e. via the categories of Scopes 1,
2 and 3 (except 3.11) and the permanent neutralization of any residual emissions. The net-zero target is
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
therefore stated as follows:
WATER AND MARINE Henkel commits to reduce absolute Scope 1, Scope 2 and Scope 3 GHG emissions by 90 percent by 2045
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
from a 2021 base year.1
BIODIVERSITY AND
Accordingly, Henkel is following the mitigation hierarchy, by targeting at least 90 percent absolute emission
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
reduction through abatement across at least 95 percent of all Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions and 90 per-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR cent of all Scope 3 emissions by 2045. To neutralize any residual emissions of a maximum of 10 percent of
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) total emissions, Henkel is following the SBTi guidelines and plans to use permanent carbon removal and
storage, with the Company relying on high-quality carbon offsets along the value chain and beyond. In the
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
future, Henkel will set milestones for neutralization to prove the integrity of our commitment to neutralize
unabated emissions at net-zero.

Ecodesign:
FOREWORD
Henkel’s transition plan requires the development of a low-emission product portfolio through optimizing
SCOPE AND REFERENCE properties and composition of products. Two levers in particular were identified here in order to achieve
FRAMEWORK emissions reductions: format design and packaging design.
GENERAL DISCLOSURES  Format design:
(ESRS 2) The optimization of product formats is an important aspect in the development of a low-emission prod-
uct portfolio. Switching to more concentrated formulations and more innovative product formats can
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
enable the use of raw materials that generate less emissions when they are produced. This can result in
emissions being reduced in the upstream value chain. In addition, further emissions reductions can be
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
achieved by a reduced amount of packaging materials used.
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)  Packaging design:
Innovative packaging designs that enable circularity are of great significance to our emissions reduction
BIODIVERSITY AND
efforts in the downstream value chain. The development of reusable and refillable packaging solutions for
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
consumers and customers allows the lifetime of the packaging materials to be extended. This results in
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR less packaging material and a reduction of the associated emissions.

Low-emission materials:
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) The materials procured for manufacturing our products have a significant influence on our GHG emissions
footprint. For this reason, the transition to more sustainable and low-emission materials is an important pre-
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS requisite for the net-zero transformation. In this regard, there are three critically important levers for emis-
(ESRS S2)
sions reduction: supplier engagement, packaging and raw materials.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES  Supplier engagement:
(ESRS S3)
Henkel considers collaboration with suppliers to be a decisive prerequisite for a holistic transformation
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS of the emissions profile of procured materials. Within the scope of the supplier engagement, the business
(ESRS S4) units together with the Henkel Purchasing team work closely with strategic suppliers and actively get in-
volved in direct abatement initiatives. This involves the development of low-emission raw materials and
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
the optimization of the respective process emissions through actions on energy efficiency and renewable
energy, and further possibilities for reducing emissions throughout the supply chain. To use the knowledge
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
relating to our upstream value chain, emission data is increasingly being integrated into the initial supplier
selection. This means it is possible to base the selection on strategic criteria that optimize the emissions
profile, for example by selecting suppliers based on specific geographical considerations.

VALUE CHAIN WORKERS optimized energy use in the upstream value chain. In addition, we aim to lower dependency on fossil fuels
(ESRS S2)

(ESRS S3)

(ESRS S4)

tive effect on the transformation of the chemical industry through its supplier management approach. In

addition to using sustainable raw materials in chemical processes – such as recycled materials or renewa-

native, low-energy production methods and their market readiness. The capture and storage or use of
CO2 in the upstream value chain can also lead to emission reductions under certain conditions.

raw materials and goods.

Policies related to climate change mitigation and adaptation (E1-2)
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
Key content of the policy (E1-2_24, MDR-P_65a, AR 16, AR 17, AR 18)
(ESRS S3)
Emissions of carbon dioxide and other greenhouse gases (GHG) caused by humans are largely responsible
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS for increased climate change and global warming. The United Nation’s global Paris Agreement on climate
(ESRS S4) change represents a commitment by the community of nations to limit global warming to well below 2 de-
grees Celsius above pre-industrial levels and to continue efforts to limit the temperature increase even fur-
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
ther to 1.5 degrees Celsius. To achieve this, GHG emissions must be reduced to net-zero by 2050. The 6th
Assessment Report (2021) of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) has confirmed that
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
climate change is already affecting every region on earth. Its impacts are increasingly visible in the form of
extreme weather, worsened droughts and heightened risk of forest fires.
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 141
As a globally operating company, Henkel acknowledges its responsibility to reduce its impact on climate
FOREWORD
change and to adapt to climate change to ensure a long-term sustainable business model. To achieve this,
SCOPE AND REFERENCE the Company is aligning its strategy with the 1.5-degree Celsius target of the Paris Climate Agreement. In
FRAMEWORK this context, Henkel commits to reaching net-zero GHG emissions across the value chain by 2045 and to
neutralizing any residual GHG emissions released into the atmosphere after 2045. This includes a stepwise
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
reduction of our absolute direct and indirect GHG emissions (including biogenic emissions resulting from
(ESRS 2)
land-use changes and the extraction of biological raw materials for the production of bioenergy), followed
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) by counterbalancing the impact of any remaining emissions in line with the SBTi Corporate Net-Zero Standard.
In order to achieve these emission reduction targets, Henkel is committed to continuously increasing its energy
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) efficiency and transitioning to renewable energies (especially electricity) while not investing in the expansion
of fossil fuels. We also want our products and technologies to contribute to reducing or, depending on the
WATER AND MARINE
application, avoiding the generation of GHG emissions. In terms of our brands and technologies, we manufacture
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
products that are associated with consumption of energy when they are used, such as detergents, shower gels
BIODIVERSITY AND and hot-melt adhesives. We want to contribute to reducing our customers‘ and consumers’ energy consumption
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) and the associated GHG footprint through innovations for greater energy efficiency.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR With this, we address the material impacts, risks and opportunities related to “climate change mitigation” in
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
our upstream and downstream value chain as well as in our own operations, and those related to “energy
use” in our downstream value chain.
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
The Sustainability Council, supported by a Net-Zero Steering Committee, regularly reviews the effectiveness
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
of our policies and progress toward our objectives as well as the material impacts, risks and opportunities
(ESRS S2)
and their changes.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
Scope (E1-2_24, MDR-P_65b)
(ESRS S3)
The provisions in this policy apply to the Henkel Group worldwide, including all operations and subsidiaries
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS as well as their upstream and downstream value chain. No business units, regions, specific activities or stake-
(ESRS S4) holders are excluded.
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) Accountability for the implementation (E1-2_24, MDR-P_65c)
The Henkel Management Board bears overall responsibility for our sustainability policies. The Sustainability
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION Council aligns on common positions, prior to approval by the Management Board. These include new strate-
gic priorities, targets as well as risks and opportunities with respect to climate change. The Sustainability
Council adopts climate-specific measures and sets up topic-specific project groups to steer the implementa-
tion of action plans, compliance with common standards, the exchange of best practices, and the monitoring
of results. It also handles cross-unit projects and continuously reviews the extent to which climate mitigation
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 142
targets are met. The business units Executive Committees (Adhesive Technologies and Consumer Brands)
FOREWORD
and relevant corporate functions are responsible for the implementation of climate concepts and policies,
SCOPE AND REFERENCE planning of relevant actions as well as for the provision of necessary resources. They are advised by their
FRAMEWORK sustainability teams.
