(ESRS 2) Henkel supports the global net-zero imperative to limit global warming to 1.5 degrees Celsius as defined
in the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change’s (IPCC) Special Report on Global Warming. This is in line
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
with our support for the guidance and ambitions of the Kyoto Protocol and the Paris Climate Agreement.
With our actions, we aim to contribute to the nationally determined contributions (NDC) of countries in which
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
we operate.
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) Henkel recognizes the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) of the United Nations as crucial goals toward
climate change mitigation, especially SDG 7 “Clean and Affordable Energy,” SDG 11 “Sustainable Cities and
BIODIVERSITY AND Communities” and SDG 13 “Climate Action.” Henkel also recognizes the GHG Protocol Corporate Accounting
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
and Reporting Standard, the GHG Protocol Policy and Action Standard (for policies and strategies) and the
GHG Protocol for Project Accounting (for projects), as the basis of our GHG emission accounting at the overall
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) company level. Our target setting, target review and target progress tracking are in line with the SBTi Corporate
Net-Zero Standard. This standard is the reference standard that harmonizes the achievement of net-zero
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) targets with social and climate-related sustainability targets within the planetary boundaries.

Taking remedial actions (E1-3_28, MDR-A_68d)
GENERAL DISCLOSURES The impacts identified by Henkel as material arise in complex ways. Since the extent of Henkel’s contribution
(ESRS 2) to such impacts cannot usually be determined with certainty, the actions taken should not be understood
as remedial actions for those who have actually been harmed by material impacts. Rather, the actions taken
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
by Henkel represent a contribution to mitigating or avoiding impacts. Such actions, including preventive
measures, encompass, for example, technical and managerial instruments, including process instructions,
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
implementation of codes, standards and guidelines, as well as accompanying training.

The key performance indicators and CapEx plan according to the EU Taxonomy Regulation
FOREWORD
(E-1_3-29c ii, 29 c iii)
The EU Taxonomy Regulation uses a much narrower definition of operating expenditures (OpEx), which
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK deviates from the traditional understanding of the term in standard accounting practice. In particular, the
definition of the operating expenditures according to the EU Taxonomy excludes certain elements such as
GENERAL DISCLOSURES the costs of the implemented services, which are generally included in the more comprehensive, generally
(ESRS 2)
accepted interpretation of operating expenditures in the financial reporting and analysis and are also used
here. The operating expenditures defined according to the EU Taxonomy Regulation are insignificant to
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
Henkel’s business model and, therefore, are not reported. For further information, please refer to the chapter
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) on the EU Taxonomy Regulation. For this reason, no correlation can be made with the operating expendi-
tures used for the transition plan.
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) Henkel’s revenue-generating economic activities are not covered by the Taxonomy Regulation. For this
reason, assets or processes that relate to revenue-generating economic activities or are part of a capital ex-
BIODIVERSITY AND penditure plan, are currently not relevant according to section 1.1.2.2 a) and b) of Annex I of the Delegated
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
Regulation on reporting requirements. Only capital expenditures relating to the purchase of output from
Taxonomy-aligned economic activities and individual measures enabling the target activities to become low-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) carbon or to lead to greenhouse gas reductions, are reported in accordance with section 1.1.2.2. c) of Annex I
of the Delegated Regulation on reporting requirements.
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
Since the necessary capital expenditures are not considered significant for the transition plan from a finan-
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS cial perspective, no connection can be established to the corresponding performance indicators of the EU
(ESRS S2)
Taxonomy.

For this reason, Henkel undertakes to achieve net-zero GHG emissions throughout the entire value chain by

(ESRS 2) Methodologies and significant assumptions (E1-4_32, MDR-T_80f, AR 24, AR 25, AR 26)
Henkel’s GHG emission reduction targets are geared toward the methodologies and SBTi climate scenario
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) analysis to define emissions reduction in pathways that are geared toward a global net-zero target before
2050. This occurs in a way that is compatible with societal climate and sustainability targets and the biophys-
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
ical limitations of the planet. To achieve a net-zero condition at company level, Henkel must significantly
reduce emissions and offset the impacts of the remaining emissions. The SBTi Corporate Net-Zero standard
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) defines net-zero emissions at the company level as follows: Emissions reduction from Scopes 1, 2 and 3 to
zero or to a residual level that is compatible with achieving net-zero emissions at the global or sectoral level
BIODIVERSITY AND in permissible 1.5-degree Celsius paths. Permanent neutralization of all residual emissions in the net-zero
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
target year and all GHG emissions released into the atmosphere afterwards are associated with this. This is in
line with the EU Green Deal and the European targets for 2030, 2040 and 2050, with which the EU aims to
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) achieve climate neutrality.
Scientific evidence (E1-4_32, MDR-T_80g, AR 24, AR 25, AR 26)
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
Henkel uses the SBTi Corporate Net-Zero Standard for defining its net-zero targets at company level in line
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS with climate science. The standard contains guidelines, criteria and recommendations that companies need
(ESRS S2) in order to define science-based net-zero targets that are in line with limiting the global temperature in-
crease to 1.5 degrees Celsius. The SBTi Corporate Net-Zero Standard is based on a science-based definition
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
of net-zero.

How GHG emission reduction targets and/or other targets are set (E1-4 _33)
WATER AND MARINE
Henkel’s net-zero targets are set in such a way that the transition risks and negative impacts will be reduced
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
throughout the value chain. They create opportunities by allowing science-based emissions reductions to
BIODIVERSITY AND be validated. With the reduction of the total emissions in all areas, Henkel aims to reduce its overall negative
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) impacts on climate change. With a lower emissions profile that is in line with the developments toward a
1.5-degree Celsius path, Henkel is reducing its global exposure to financial transition risks such as the intro-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
duction of carbon taxation and market restrictions, which will have a negative impact on the costs for raw
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
materials, packaging and transportation. These risks can manifest themselves in the form of higher prices for
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) raw materials, packaging and transportation services, as well as a possible shortage of goods and services.

(ESRS S3)
Scopes 1, 2 and 3 excluding category 3.11. Biogenic CO2 emissions from incineration or biodegradation of

CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
(ESRS S4) other GHG types (especially CH4 and N2O). They remain within the Scopes 1, 2 and 3 according to the GHG
Protocol. Since Henkel follows the SBTi Net-Zero Standard, the removal of greenhouse gases, CO2 certificates

GHG emission reduction targets (E1-4_34a, 34b, AR 27, AR 28, AR 29)
FOREWORD
Henkel’s GHG emissions reduction targets meet the following requirements: They are given in absolute
SCOPE AND REFERENCE values and cover all the relevant GHG emissions of Scopes 1, 2 and 3. Henkel indicates the combined GHG
FRAMEWORK emission reduction targets in the table “GHG emissions,” such as information about which GHG emissions
(Scope 1, 2 and/or 3) are covered by each target, the share related to each respective Scope and which
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
greenhouse gases are covered. To present its Scope 1 and 2 GHG emissions, Henkel uses accounting accord-
(ESRS 2)
ing to a market-based approach and a location-based approach, and lists them in separate lines. By setting
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) targets that consider all relevant greenhouse gases and the relevant Scopes and aims for absolute reduc-
tions compared to the base year without using climate compensation certificates, the coherence of these
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) targets with the limitations of our greenhouse gas inventory is ensured.
WATER AND MARINE Representativeness of the baseline value for progress measurement (E1-4_AR 25a)
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) Henkel has chosen 2021 as the base year because it is representative of our average business activities. After
GHG emissions reversed in some Scopes in 2020 due to the global economic downturn from lockdowns as-
BIODIVERSITY AND
sociated with the COVID-19 pandemic, emissions increased again in 2021, which can be seen as representative
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
for a normalized fiscal year. Cessation of business activities in Russia in the first quarter of 2022 was retro-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR spectively taken into consideration in the recalculation of the baseline value. The suitability of the base year
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) was validated by the SBTi during the course of the target validation process.
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) Scientific basis and the use of scenarios (E1-4_34e)
Henkel’s SBTi climate targets for 2030 and the SBTi net-zero target were defined according to guidelines,
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS criteria and recommendations of the SBTi Corporate Net-Zero Standard and officially validated by the SBTi.
(ESRS S2)
The targets are therefore in line with the restriction of the global temperature increase to 1.5 degrees Celsius.
At the time of target validation there was no finalized version of the sector-specific guidelines for the chemi-
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) cals sector, so Henkel used the guidelines of the cross-sectoral pathway of the SBTi. Accordingly, the targets
are aimed at the respective base climate and policy scenarios that are considered by the SBTi. The emission
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS reduction pathways consider the availability of low-carbon energy and low-carbon/reduced GHG emissions
(ESRS S4)
materials, packaging and technologies, in particular from the various availability scenarios such as materials
within the chemicals sector. The transformation of this sector is of particular importance. The availability of
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
green energies and low-carbon raw materials such as renewable or recycled raw materials are of central rele-
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION vance. The increased demand for low-carbon products and the associated expected revenue increases were
taken into account. This represents a material opportunity that was identified in the materiality assessment.
That is why it is a material factor for setting our targets. However, expected regulatory changes represent a
material financial risk as outlined in the materiality assessment which can be reduced by Henkel’s net-zero
target setting. Our product and packaging strategy and the general principles of ecodesign, as presented in
Henkel’s transition plan, were considered when developing the expected volume growth.

Henkel uses various studies focusing on some of the most important commercial sectors that the Company
works together with for procurement. In this way, we aim to better understand the prices and availability of
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
certain pertinent materials within the scope of developing CO2 pricing and guaranteeing cost competitive-
WATER AND MARINE ness. The CO2 pricing model is closely linked to climate scenarios because Henkel assumes that the introduc-
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) tion or expansion of CO2 pricing systems is likely to increase with global warming. Henkel assumes that after
2030, significantly more levers and technologies driving net-zero will be available. For 2030, Henkel is using
BIODIVERSITY AND
the levers available in 2024, which are not linked to the scenarios of climate change, and its influence on the
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
availability of technologies, scalability or the price competitiveness.

Metrics used (MDR-M_75)
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) The ESRS E1 standard for climate change requires disclosure of certain metrics for climate change mitigation
and energy use, especially the measurement of energy consumption in megawatt hours (MWh) and the
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS measurement of GHG emissions in metric tons of CO2 equivalents. These metrics evaluate the direct and
(ESRS S2)
indirect impacts on the climate. The risks that have been determined in connection with these metrics are
associated directly with the reduction of GHG emissions. In particular, the reduction of GHG emissions reduces
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) the potential burden of climate-related taxes, fees and regulatory restrictions.

Gross Scopes 1, 2, 3 and total GHG emissions (E1-6)
FOREWORD
Methodologies, significant assumptions and emission factors (E1-6_AR 39b)
SCOPE AND REFERENCE For Scope 1, our methodology follows the GHG Protocol Corporate Accounting and Reporting Standard. For
FRAMEWORK energy-related emissions, we use DEFRA emissions factors (Department for Environment, Food and Rural
Affairs) to convert activity data (such as fuel consumption) to CO₂-equivalent emissions. For Kyoto Protocol
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
gases, the formula for calculating process emissions from non-energy-related sources is used: “GHG = amount
(ESRS 2)
of emissions x global warming potential (GWP).” We also use the formula “GHG = amount of emissions x
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) GWP” for refrigerant emissions.
According to the GHG Protocol Scope 2 Corporate Accounting and Reporting Standard, the Scope 2 emis-
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
sions are calculated according to a hierarchical approach. The hierarchy orders the emission factors from the
WATER AND MARINE most specific (supplier-specific) to the broadest (country averages from the International Energy Agency, IEA).
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) Scope 3 emissions are either quantified using primary data (see the table “CO2e emissions – Explanations”)
or are determined using recognized methodologies such as green/environmental accounting (Life Cycle
BIODIVERSITY AND
Assessments, LCA) or environmentally extended input-output (EEIO) and internal calculations. The Scope 3
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
emissions are quantified using the calculation according to the equation in the GHG Protocol Scope 3 Cor-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR porate Accounting and Reporting Standard: “GHG = activity data x emission factor x GWP.”
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
The GWP data taken as the basis for Scopes 1, 2 and 3 is aligned with the current IPCC report with a time
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) horizon of 100 years. In a few cases, GWP data from older IPCC progress reports or sources was used. To
calculate Scope 3.6 emissions (business trips), the GWP data of the fifth IPCC report is used for air travel,
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
according to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). For refrigerants
(ESRS S2)
(Scope 1) the following applies: If no GWP is recorded in the IPCC report for a substance, reference is made
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES instead to data of the California Air Resources Board. The methodologies, significant assumptions and
(ESRS S3) emission factors that were used for calculating the GHG emissions and the reasons why they were chosen,
including the calculation tools and external databases used for the calculation, are provided in detail in
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
the table “CO2e emissions – Explanations.“
(ESRS S4)

Henkel’s GHG emissions are presented in the table “GHG emissions.“ The emissions are broken down ac-
SCOPE AND REFERENCE cording to Scope 1, 2 and 3 including all sub-categories. Scope 1 emissions have also been additionally
FRAMEWORK broken down to indicate Henkel’s production (including electricity and steam generation, and also process
emissions) and energy production for third parties. Scope 3 emissions have been further broken down in
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
principle into the 15 categories, while category 3.1 has been further broken down into the categories of raw
(ESRS 2)
materials, packaging and other Scope 3.1 emissions. Categories 3.8, 3.10, 3.13 and 3.14 are not shown in the
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) table because they are insignificant for our business model according to our significance analysis.

WATER AND MARINE statements in the reporting period. No material events or changes to the circumstances occurred in the
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) reporting year. The only category for which GHG emissions from the previous year are reported is category
3.15 – investments.

RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
1 because we consider this information to be material for the transparency of our direct energy use and

have reported this information in previous years. The GHG emissions of Scopes 1, 2 and 3 and the biogenic
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) emissions of Scope 1 are presented in the table “GHG emissions.“ In doing so, the biogenic emissions only
include the direct CO2 emissions from the incineration or biodegradation of biomass. Other greenhouse
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS gases (especially N2O or CH4) remain in Scope 1.

CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) Use of site-based and market-based methods for calculating Scope 2 GHG emissions (E1-6_AR 45d)
In 2024, Henkel used contractual instruments for market-related calculation of its Scope 2 GHG emissions.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) The instruments include virtual agreements relating to the acquisition of electricity, eco tariffs and unbun-
dled energy attribute certificates. In 2024, the share of acquired electricity compared to overall acquired
WATER AND MARINE electricity was at a) 53 percent for virtual agreements, b) was at 28 percent for eco tariffs and c) at 6 percent
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
for unbundled energy attribute certificates. The remaining 13 percent of our consumption from purchased
or received electricity, heat, steam or cooling from renewable sources (MWh) is not assigned an attribute or
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) an attribute relationship cannot be established
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR Breakdown of Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions (E1-6_50)
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) The same scope of consolidation applies for the reporting of gross Scope 1 and Scope 2 GHG emissions
as applies for the consolidated financial statements. Henkel has neither operational control of the associated

FRAMEWORK Internal carbon pricing (E1-8)
Type of internal carbon pricing scheme (E1-8 _63a, AR 66)
GENERAL DISCLOSURES Internal carbon prices can be an effective instrument to link investments to sustainable solutions. Henkel
(ESRS 2) uses shadow carbon prices for relevant business decisions in order to anticipate and mitigate risks from an
increasing carbon price and to create incentives for emissions reductions. This strategy goes hand in hand
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
with Henkel’s net-zero targets. The company does not use any internal carbon fees.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
Specific scope of application of the carbon pricing schemes (E1-8_63b, AR 66)
Shadow pricing is used in particular for strategic questions when setting targets, for the further development
WATER AND MARINE
of raw materials and product portfolios, as well as for the supply of energy.
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
Carbon prices applied and critical assumptions for price determination (E1-8_63c, AR 66)
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
A global average CO2 price was calculated for the shadow price model based on the weighted average of
the national and regional CO2 price, in which the latest data from the World Bank was used. To calculate the
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR weighting factors, Henkel’s GHG emissions in the respective countries were used. The global average calcu-
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) lated for the reporting year 2024 amounted to USD 19.1 per CO2 metric ton. The extrapolation of the CO2
price was based on the World Energy Outlook of the IEA and its “Net Zero Emissions by 2050“ scenario.

Pollution (ESRS E2)
FOREWORD
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK Strategy
Material impacts, risks and opportunities and their interaction with strategy and business model
GENERAL DISCLOSURES (ESRS 2, SBM-3_48b)
(ESRS 2)
Our business and industry customers, as well as consumers and end-users, rightfully expect that our products
are safe when they are used as intended. That is why we undertake to comply with all statutory requirements.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
This includes consistently following locally imposed prohibitions or restrictions about the use of substances
that are classified by regulators as “Substances of Very High Concern” (SVHCs) because of possible negative
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
impacts on people and the environment. In addition, we follow our Safety, Health and Environmental Protec-
WATER AND MARINE tion (SHE) standards and ensure that Henkel’s products can be used without having negative impacts on
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
human health or the environment. This also applies to products that contain SVHCs. We also implement
policies and actions to minimize the use of SVHCs, and have set ourselves the goal of replacing SVHCs with
BIODIVERSITY AND
alternative solutions. To achieve this, Henkel works with all of the relevant stakeholders along the value chain.
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
A small share of Henkel’s net revenue is generated by products that contain SVHCs, mostly in low concentra-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) tions of below 1 percent. This includes substances that are used in low amounts because of their technical
characteristics, such as UV stabilizers and antioxidants. It also includes substances that are contained in our
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) raw materials as by-products or contaminants. Potential statutory restrictions on the use of these substances
in the future could negatively influence the sale of products containing SVHCs and cause increased costs for
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
adjustment actions. Consequently, potential restrictions in the future for Henkel are associated with financial
(ESRS S2)
risks in its downstream value chain.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
Impact, risk and opportunity management
(ESRS S3)
Policies related to pollution (E2-1)
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS Key content of the policy (E2-1_14, MDR-P_65a; E2-1_15b; E2-1_AR 12)
(ESRS S4) In connection with the topic of environmental pollution, it is our commitment to comply with external re-
quirements worldwide. Henkel therefore complies with local regulations for substances of very high concern
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
(SVHC) worldwide. In addition to the SVHC definition of the European Sustainability Reporting Standards
(ESRS), we also take local definitions into account. By complying with our internal standards, we ensure that
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
Henkel products can be used without having a negative impact on human health or the environment. This
also applies to products that contain substances of very high concern.
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 172
In addition, we strive to increase safety during the manufacturing and use phase as well as for end-users.
FOREWORD
As part of product development, both divisions implement global portfolio management initiatives. This
SCOPE AND REFERENCE involves proactively developing solutions to replace products containing SVHCs without compromising the
FRAMEWORK quality and efficiency of the product solution for customers. We are doing this because reducing emissions
from the process is less expensive and less demanding from a technical point of view when using products
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
without SVHCs. These advantages can also be a benefit to our customers.
(ESRS 2)
Within these initiatives, programs are developed for reformulating or replacing products that are flagged as
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
containing SVHC. Due to the different characteristics of the portfolios of Henkel’s two business units, their
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) respective policies have different priorities. The raw material strategy of Consumer Brands takes into account
that many of the products pass into wastewater after use – such as detergents and cleaning agents, as well
WATER AND MARINE as hair and personal care products. For that reason, the Consumer Brands raw material strategy has a focus
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
on (bio)degradable ingredients. The strategy pursued by Adhesive Technologies for the responsible use of
chemicals (“Responsible Chemistry”) is embedded in the sustainable portfolio assessment process of this
BIODIVERSITY AND
business unit. In line with this strategy, Adhesive Technologies proactively replaces products containing SVHC
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
with SVHC-free alternatives. This has advantages for our customers.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) Henkel’s policy for minimizing the use of SVHC consists of four elements:
 Henkel complies with local laws and regulations that prohibit these substances or restrict their use and
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
concentration in a product.
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS  In cases where it is not yet possible to eliminate SVHC, Henkel ensures that products containing SVHC
(ESRS S2) can be used safely by providing instructions on the proper use of these products.
 Henkel anticipates future regulations and works with stakeholders to identify the need for alternative
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
solutions for products containing SVHC.
(ESRS S3)
 In cases where alternatives without SVHCs are available, Henkel actively engages with stakeholders along
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS the value chain to encourage them to switch to these alternatives.
(ESRS S4)
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 173
The policy is in line with the “zero pollution hierarchy” of the EU action plan to prevent pollution of air, water
FOREWORD
and soil. The first two concept elements mentioned, ʼregulatory complianceʽ and ʼproduct safety,ʽ address
SCOPE AND REFERENCE the hierarchy level ʼMinimize and Control.ʽ The concept elements listed in third and fourth place deal with
FRAMEWORK alternatives in the sense of ʼproactive transformation of products & solutions.ʽ They are assigned to the
ʼPreventʽ hierarchy level. All concept elements introduce measures to minimize the most harmful chemicals
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
in consumer products and in products for professional users.
(ESRS 2)
With this, we address the material risks related to SVHC in our downstream value chain. Portfolio management
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
monitors the progress of specific initiatives to reduce products containing SVHCs in the two business units
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) and reports to the Consumer Brands and Adhesive Technologies Executive Committees. The Sustainability
Council regularly reviews the effectiveness of our policies and progress toward our objectives, as well as the
WATER AND MARINE material impacts, risks and opportunities and their changes.
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
Scope (E2-1_14, MDR-P_65b)
BIODIVERSITY AND The provisions in this policy apply to the Henkel Group worldwide, including all operations and subsidiaries
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
as well as their upstream and downstream value chain. No business units, regions, specific activities or stake-
holders are excluded. Therefore, this policy includes Henkel´s entire product portfolio.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Accountability for the implementation (E2-1_14, MDR-P_65c)
The Henkel Management Board bears overall responsibility for our sustainability concepts and policies.
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
Guided by a corporate expert, strategic topic leaders for pollution in the business units are responsible for
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS the development of concepts and policies addressing SVHC. They are responsible for their governance and
(ESRS S2) lead the implementation of relevant measures based on company-wide standards and programs. The two
business units are responsible for implementing the remaining concept elements. This includes providing
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
the necessary resources. In Adhesive Technologies the leaders of the strategic business units have the re-
(ESRS S3)
sponsibility for implementing these policy elements. In Consumer Brands, the members of the executive
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS committee take this responsibility. The effectiveness of our policies and progress toward our objectives as
(ESRS S4) well as the material impacts, risks and opportunities and their changes are regularly reviewed by the Sustain-
ability Council.

Commitment to third-party standards or initiatives (E2-1_14, MDR-P_65d)
FOREWORD
We ensure that Henkel makes its contribution to achieving the goals of the ‘Global Framework on Chemicals’
SCOPE AND REFERENCE of the United Nations Environment Program. In doing so, we follow the legal requirements and are committed
FRAMEWORK to avoiding SVHCs.
GENERAL DISCLOSURES Consideration of stakeholders (E2-1_14, MDR-P_65e)
(ESRS 2) We maintain dialog and consider the interests of all our stakeholders. The transformation of products is
shaped by intensive dialog with customers. This enables our teams to learn more about the needs of our
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
customers with the aim of minimizing potential exposure to SVHCs.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) Accessibility and communication (E2-1_14, MDR-P_65f)
This policy is made available to all employees and external stakeholders through our Code of Sustainability
WATER AND MARINE
on our website (henkel.com), within the Downloads & Publications section. The same applies to our SHE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
standards with further details on product safety. In addition, Henkel has fully formalized its methodology for
BIODIVERSITY AND sustainable portfolio assessment. Those stakeholders in the Product Development, Product Safety and Sus-

Water and marine resources (ESRS E3)
FOREWORD
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK Strategy
Material impacts, risks and opportunities and their interaction with strategy and business model
GENERAL DISCLOSURES (ESRS 2, SBM-3_48b)
(ESRS 2)
Water plays a crucial role along our value chain. We use water as a part of our production processes and as
an ingredient in our products. Many of our products also require water during their use phase. Water con-
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
sumption and water withdrawal throughout the value chain represent material environmental impacts for
Henkel. Negative impacts on nature related to water are linked to Henkel’s business model as a consumer
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
goods company (laundry and home care, hair and body care), as well as to its adhesives, sealants and coatings.
WATER AND MARINE In regions already affected by water scarcity, water resources may come under additional pressure. A lack of
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
water availability and quality can harm human health and damage ecosystems. For Henkel as a company,
there is a potential risk that water scarcity may jeopardize ongoing operations in water-stressed regions,
BIODIVERSITY AND
which may lead to temporary business interruptions and revenue losses.
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
We recognize the critical role water plays in our operations, supply chains and communities. As part of our
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) strategic direction, we are also addressing water-related issues and are aiming for sustainable use of water
resources along our value chain. To this end, we have set the target of reducing water withdrawals by
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) 35 percent per metric ton of product by 2025, using 2010 as the base year. This strategic target requires
adjustments to Henkel’s business model, both in our operations and across the downstream value chain.

(ESRS S2)

Impact, risk and opportunity management
FOREWORD
Policies related to water and marine resources (E3-1)
SCOPE AND REFERENCE Key content of the policy (E3-1_11, MDR-P_65a, E3-1_12a, E3-1_12a i, E3-1_12a ii, E3-1_12b, 12c, E3-1_13)
FRAMEWORK Water is a finite resource and is essential for society, economy, and nature. Research on the planetary
boundaries indicates that human-induced modification of the earth systems has led to the transgression of
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
the freshwater change boundary. Marine resources are essential to natural ecosystems and the human food
(ESRS 2)
chain. As a globally operating company, Henkel acknowledges its responsibility for actions on water scarcity
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) and the protection of aquatic ecosystems and marine resources. In this context, Henkel aims for holistic water
stewardship that enables responsible water management across the value chain based on collective action.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) This includes reduction of water consumption, circular water use and engagement in water-replenishment
projects.
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
We promote sustainable water management in our operational processes, primarily by reducing water
withdrawal and consumption. We apply efficiency measures and the reuse of wastewater wherever possible.
BIODIVERSITY AND
We apply wastewater treatment to avoid water pollution resulting from our operational activities as well as
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
to enable water recycling. Pretreatments are applied when required to remove any pollutants prior to treat-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR ment in the conventional wastewater treatment.
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
When sourcing water, sustainable and environmentally compatible methods are prioritized. This includes
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) sourcing water from renewable and responsibly managed supplies, avoiding over-extraction from vulnerable
ecosystems, and preferring sources that have a minimal impact on local communities and wildlife. We strive
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
to reduce water consumption in areas at water risk in our own operations and across our value chain. Inno-
(ESRS S2)
vations in product design are key to sustainable water management, as they optimize water requirements in
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES production and reduce the water consumption of our products during the use phase. Innovations in product
(ESRS S3) design are also used to prevent water pollution in the downstream value chain. In doing so, we aim to replace
substances that have a negative impact on people or nature without compromising the quality and efficiency
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
of the solutions. To promote the responsible use of water in the upstream value chain, we strive to encourage
(ESRS S4)
our suppliers to improve their practices in the areas of water conservation, quality monitoring, wastewater
treatment and recycling.
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)

WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) Key current and planned actions (E3-2_17, MDR-A_68a, AR 22)
To advance Henkel’s commitment to sustainable and circular water usage, three primary actions are being
BIODIVERSITY AND implemented in relation to freshwater use and marine ecosystems. These are:
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
 Actions to increase water efficiency
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR  Actions for circular water usage
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)  Consumer engagement
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) The actions for water efficiency and circular water usage aim to reduce the identified material impacts of our
operations on water consumption and withdrawal. Both actions align with the goals of Henkel’s Code of
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
Sustainability, which include reducing water withdrawals and freshwater consumption in Henkel’s operations.
(ESRS S2)
Henkel is committed to reducing water withdrawals (surface water, groundwater and third-party water) by
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES 35 percent by 2025, using 2010 as the base year. Water efficiency actions help to prevent water loss and reduce
(ESRS S3) the total amount of water used in operations. This can be achieved, for example, by optimizing the changeover
sequence to minimize cleaning water, implementing efficient cleaning systems, and introducing closed-loop
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
cooling systems to reduce the amount of water required for cooling. Circular water usage actions facilitate
(ESRS S4)
the treatment and reuse of water within the operational boundary. (Waste-)water is used more than once
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) (treated or untreated) in the same or in another process in Henkel’s own operations. Examples include reusing
and recycling cleaning water in production processes or the recycling of water in sanitation processes. Actions
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION for circular water usage also include collecting rainwater from the roofs of production facilities for use in
operational processes to reduce water withdrawal.
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 181
Consumer engagement actions align with the objectives of Henkel’s Code of Sustainability by informing users
FOREWORD
of Henkel products about sustainable water use and resource conservation. One example is our “It Starts
SCOPE AND REFERENCE with Us” program, which provides consumers with practical tips for the sustainable and water-saving use of
FRAMEWORK our products. Consumer engagement actions address the reduction of material impacts on water consumption
and withdrawal in the downstream value chain.
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
(ESRS 2)
Actions to address material impacts in the upstream value chain are being considered.

RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR To align with our ambition for sustainable water usage, Henkel has set a target to reduce water withdrawal

Progress toward the target is measured against a baseline value of 1.23 m3 of water withdrawal per metric
SCOPE AND REFERENCE ton of product (8,514,105 m3/6,894,844 tons of product). The base year is 2010.
FRAMEWORK
Period (E3-3_22, MDR-T_80e, AR 24, AR 25, AR 26)
GENERAL DISCLOSURES The period for achieving the target spans from the base year 2010 to the target year 2025. No milestones or
(ESRS 2) interim targets have been defined.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) Methodologies and significant assumptions (E3-3_22, MDR-T_80f, AR 24, AR 2, AR 26)
The target is based on scientific insights into planetary boundaries and on the United Nations Environment
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) Program (UNEP) finding that water demand is projected to exceed supply by up to 40 percent by 2030. In
addition, it refers to the social relevance of water and sanitation for the world’s population in a broader con-
WATER AND MARINE
text. No additional significant assumptions were made when defining the target.
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
Scientific evidence (E3-3_22, MDR-T_80g, AR 24, AR 25, AR 26)
BIODIVERSITY AND
The target is rooted in the policy of planetary boundaries, particularly those related to freshwater systems.
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
This planetary boundary highlights the deviation of surface water conditions from their pre-industrial state.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR This planetary boundary was already exceeded in 2023, which leads to the conclusion that a target must be
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) set for water withdrawal reduction.

Internally, stakeholders involved in the target-setting process included employees from Henkel’s operations,
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS sustainability managers from the business units, site managers and the global Sustainability team. An internal
(ESRS S2)
steering committee is regularly informed about the process. Targets were developed in 2021 with considera-
tion given to the perspectives of external stakeholders. Steps such as a stakeholder survey helped to ensure this.

(ESRS S3)

(ESRS S4) data collection processes since this target was established.

GENERAL DISCLOSURES
(ESRS 2) Methodologies and significant assumptions (MDR-M_77a)
The analysis of the metrics for total water withdrawal and total water consumption applies to Henkel-operated
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) production sites, offices, research, training and logistics facilities that are owned, rented or managed by the
Company. For the withdrawal intensity metric from surface water, groundwater and third-party sources in
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) m3 per metric ton of product only Henkel-operated production sites are considered. Supplier facilities are
excluded from all metrics.
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
Each site reports water withdrawal metrics monthly through a centralized reporting tool. For the total water
withdrawal metric, the following sources are included: Surface water, groundwater, seawater, produced water,
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) third-party water and rainwater. For the water intensity metric concerning total water withdrawal, only surface
water, groundwater and third-party water are considered. Total water consumption is defined as the difference
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR between total water withdrawal and total water discharge. This metric represents the total volume of water
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
withdrawn by Henkel that is not returned to the environment or third parties. Water consumption also includes
water contained in our product formulations. Total water discharge includes releases to surface water,
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
groundwater, seawater and water to third parties.
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
Areas of water stress are regions where the proportion of total water withdrawn from surface water and
(ESRS S2)
groundwater is classified as high (40–80 percent) or extremely high (over 80 percent) in relation to the available
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES renewable water.
(ESRS S3)
The World Wildlife Fund (WWF) Water Risk Filter with the respective “Baseline Water Stress” indicator was
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS used to identify locations in water-stressed areas. The underlying data on water stress is derived from the
(ESRS S4)
PCR-GLOBWB 2 data model. This model has been evaluated with geographic granularity according to Hydro-
BASINS levels 6 and 7. The data that the data model is based on covers the period from 1960 to 2014, and
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
has been extrapolated within the model to 2021.
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 186
For areas at water risk, various physical aspects are considered, including water availability, quality, quantity,
FOREWORD
accessibility and regulatory or reputational risks. The indicators “basin physical risk,” “basin reputational risk”
SCOPE AND REFERENCE and “basin regulatory risk” were used to identify areas with high or very high water risks.
FRAMEWORK
The total amount of recycled and reused water is defined as the combined volume of water and wastewater
GENERAL DISCLOSURES that has been reused multiple times within Henkel’s operational boundary before being discharged, lost,
(ESRS 2)
incorporated into a product formulation, transferred to third parties or otherwise permanently leaving the
Company. This may be in the same process (recycled) or in a different process within the same facility or
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
another of the undertaking’s facilities (reuse). Treatment of wastewater at internal or third-party treatment
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) facilities before recycling and reuse at the undertaking’s facility is allowed, if it can be proven that the
treated volumes originate from and are reused/recycled at an undertaking’s facility. The amount considers
WATER AND MARINE the number of recycles or reuses (volume of water recycled/reused multiplied by the number of cycles) and
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
thus reflects the total amount of water from other sources that was saved by reuse and recycling, thereby
reducing the Company’s water demand. Water used in closed-loop systems is excluded from this calculation,
BIODIVERSITY AND
as such systems are considered single processes rather than multiple processes or process steps the used
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
water undergoes before discharge. Each site reports these data annually using a centralized reporting tool.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) Total water stored is defined as the water reserved for exceptional circumstances at Henkel sites, such as fire
water or reserve tanks that are not used in regular production processes. To ensure only tanks of significant
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) size and relevance to exceptional situations are captured, only tanks with a capacity greater than 100 m3 are
included in this metric. Each site reports these data annually using a centralized reporting tool.

(ESRS S2) In connection with water consumption, water intensity is defined as the ratio of total water consumption

Biodiversity and ecosystems (ESRS E4)
FOREWORD
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK Strategy
Material impacts, risks and opportunities and their interaction with strategy and business model
GENERAL DISCLOSURES (ESRS 2, SBM-3_48b)
(ESRS 2)
The preservation of biodiversity and the protection of intact ecosystems are the main basis for providing
resources that are important for life and for the ability to adapt to environmental changes. As part of its
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
materiality analysis, Henkel has identified material impacts on biodiversity and ecosystems. These are cate-
gorized into direct drivers of biodiversity loss (climate change, land use change, freshwater-use changes,
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
direct exploitation of resources) and impacts on the scale and condition of ecosystems (land degradation).
WATER AND MARINE These material impacts are closely linked to Henkel’s business model as a consumer goods company (laundry
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
and home care, hair and body care), as well as to its adhesives, sealants and coatings segments. This includes
activities across all phases of the value chain that contribute to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and water
BIODIVERSITY AND
consumption. In the upstream value chain, activities may lead to land degradation and land use changes,
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
particularly through the use of land-intensive (biological) raw materials that may contribute to deforestation
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR when they are cultivated.
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Henkel acknowledges these impacts and is committed to protecting and restoring biodiversity as part of its
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) strategic orientation, with a particular focus on forests, land and water. In addition to applying these princi-
ples to the activities at our own sites, we also promote the responsible management of raw materials and
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
the use of ingredients based on renewable raw materials. The focus is particularly on the responsible sourcing
(ESRS S2)
of palm (kernel) oil and timber products, which have been identified as critical raw materials in relation to
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES deforestation.
(ESRS S3)
Activities and locations in biodiversity-sensitive areas (SBM-3_16a, 16a i, 16a ii, 16a iii)
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS Henkel has identified sites within its own operations that may materially impact nearby areas with biodi-
(ESRS S4) versity-sensitive areas (sites with relevance to biodiversity). The identification of these sites with relevance to
biodiversity is based on proximity to biodiversity-sensitive areas and the two direct drivers of biodiversity
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
loss identified in Henkel’s materiality analysis: Climate change and freshwater-use change. Direct exploitation
of water is addressed through the assessment of freshwater-use change for identifying sites relevant to bio-
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
diversity. The direct exploitation of biological resources, pollution, seawater use change and the introduction
of invasive species were not identified as material within Henkel’s own operations and were therefore not
considered in the identification of sites relevant to biodiversity.
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 190
Building on this, the following criteria must be met for a site to be classified as a site relevant to biodiversity:
FOREWORD
1. The site is located near (within a two-kilometer radius) an area with sensitive biodiversity. This radius
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK corresponds to the dimensions of Henkel’s largest production site in terms of area in Düsseldorf-Holt-
hausen, and sites that overlap onto biodiversity-sensitive areas and sites directly bordering on biodiversity-
GENERAL DISCLOSURES sensitive areas are identified. Impacts on biodiversity-sensitive areas that are further away are limited
(ESRS 2)
because Henkel adheres to existing legal requirements on emissions limits.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
2. The site is among the 10 percent of Henkel locations with the highest Scope 1 GHG emissions (impacts:
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) climate change as a driver of biodiversity loss) or is classified as a priority water site. A priority water site
is defined as a location where the physical risk of the surrounding watershed is rated as “high” or “very
WATER AND MARINE high” either currently, in 2030 (scenario based on current trend, described in the Water Risk Filter Meth-
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) odology Documentation of the World Wildlife Fund [WWF] or in 2050 (scenario based on current trend)
and where annual water withdrawal exceeds the average annual withdrawal of Henkel production sites.
BIODIVERSITY AND
The calculation of the average annual amount of water withdrawal is based on the total water withdrawal
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
of Henkel production sites divided by the number of production sites (impacts: freshwater-use change as
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR a direct cause of biodiversity loss).
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
Biodiversity-sensitive areas include Key Biodiversity Areas (KBAs) and protected areas listed in the World
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) Database of Protected Areas (WDPA). The WDPA is the most comprehensive global database of marine
and terrestrial protected areas. The criteria for identifying Key Biodiversity Areas (KBAs) are divided into five
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
categories: threatened biodiversity, geographically restricted biodiversity, ecological integrity, biological
(ESRS S2)
processes and irreplaceability.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
The impact on the environment of Henkel’s sites relevant to biodiversity was defined according to the ap-
(ESRS S3)
propriate criteria met for identification and either applies to climate change, freshwater-use change or both.
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS The relevant dependencies of sites relevant to biodiversity on ecosystem services were identified using the
(ESRS S4) WWF Biodiversity Risk Filter, using the three summarizing categories contained in the filter: “Provisioning
Services,” “Regulating and Supporting Services – Enabling” and “Regulating Services – Mitigation.” The fourth
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
category of the ecosystem services “Cultural Services” was not taken into account because it does not directly
relate to Henkel’s business activities. Dependencies were identified if the risk score for the relevant category
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
in the WWF Biodiversity Risk Filter was rated as “high” or “very high.” The ecosystem status was assessed
using the WWF Biodiversity Risk Filter, specifically the “6.4 Ecosystem Condition” indicator, which reflects the
integrity and connectivity of the ecosystem. Sites relevant to biodiversity, along with their identified impacts
on biodiversity, on-site activities, dependencies on ecosystem services, current ecosystem condition and
nearby areas of vulnerable biodiversity, are detailed in the table “Sites relevant to biodiversity.”
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 191

HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 197
Transition plan and consideration of biodiversity and ecosystems in strategy and business model
FOREWORD
(E4-1)
SCOPE AND REFERENCE Resilience of business model and risk mitigation strategy (E4-1_13a, AR 2, AR 3)
FRAMEWORK The materiality analysis performed by Henkel included a risk assessment regarding financial effects. No
material transition, physical or systemic risks related to biodiversity and ecosystems were identified. It was
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
concluded on this basis that Henkel’s business model overall is resilient to risks related to biodiversity be-
(ESRS 2)
cause no biodiversity-related risks are material for Henkel.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
Potential risks assessed as part of the materiality analysis in the upstream value chain regarding biodiversity
included rising costs and the unavailability of certain bio-based raw materials. These risks may result from
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
new legal requirements (transition risks), such as anti-deforestation regulations leading to reduced arable
WATER AND MARINE land availability, decreased pollination due to insect species extinction or the impacts of droughts or floods
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
(physical risks). These risks were not classified as material because Henkel’s portfolio is not reliant on a single
raw material, and the Company has a resilient business model through diversification.
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
For Henkel’s own operations, potential risks such as fines or penalties for non-compliance with environmental
contamination regulations were assessed as part of the materiality analysis and were classified as not material.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) Transition risks related to the need to modify packaging formats and physical risks associated with insufficient
water supply for production sites in water-scarce regions were also considered and were classified as not
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) material.
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS Resilience analysis (E4-1_13b, 13c, 13e, AR 2, AR 3)

GENERAL DISCLOSURES Policies related to biodiversity and ecosystems (E4-2)
(ESRS 2)
Key content of the policy (E4-2_22, MDR-P_65a, E4-2_23a, 23b, AR 4, E4-2_23e, E4-2_24a, 24d)
Globally, human activities have changed nature and ecosystems, including land surfaces and ocean areas.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
This has led to a rapid decline of biodiversity on earth with more than 1 million species facing extinction.
Henkel recognizes its responsibility toward the protection of biodiversity on earth and is committed to
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
protecting and restoring biodiversity with a focus on forests, land and water. The policy sets the guiding
WATER AND MARINE principles for our biodiversity targets and ambitions, as well as general principles for the selection of actions
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) and the transition strategy toward our targets and ambitions.
BIODIVERSITY AND Henkel aims to support the efforts toward the global Nature Positive goal (defined by the Nature Positive
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
Initiative and WWF and in line with the United Nations Kunming-Montreal Agreement) to strengthen resili-
ence to climate change. This support for environmental protection and restoration can extend to activities
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) along and beyond the value chain. Henkel strives to avoid negative impacts on threatened and protected
species, especially on own operation sites located in or near sensitive ecosystems. In alignment, Henkel does
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1) not trade with species listed in the Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna
and Flora (CITES) and respects legally designated protected areas.
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) Henkel is driving the transition to renewable raw materials, including recycled materials, bio-based materials
and materials derived from carbon capture and utilization. To avoid, reduce and minimize potential negative
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
impacts on the environment and people in the upstream value chain, Henkel is committed to responsible
(ESRS S3)
sourcing of raw materials. Henkel is also committed to zero net deforestation and has an ambition of defor-
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS estation- and conversion-free sourcing of high-volume commodities with a high risk for deforestation, con-
(ESRS S4) version, or human rights violations. This applies in particular to timber, pulp and paper, palm oil, palm kernel
oil and their derivatives, and other commodities falling under relevant national and international legislation.
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 199
Henkel’s deforestation and conversion-free ambition for relevant commodities includes transparency along
FOREWORD
the value chain, no conversion of natural ecosystems to agriculture or other land uses, no burning or use of
SCOPE AND REFERENCE fire for land clearing/replanting, and a zero net deforestation or DCF (deforestation- and conversion-free) or
FRAMEWORK NDPE (No Deforestation, No Peat and No Exploitation) commitment of suppliers. This is in line with legal
requirements, such as the European Deforestation Directive (EUDR), but goes beyond national and regional
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
regulations due to the global scope of the concept. Henkel strives to minimize the direct drivers of biodiver-
(ESRS 2)
sity loss in our own operations, such as the reduction of production waste, waste to landfill, air, water and
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) soil emissions, as well as reduction of freshwater consumption. In addition, we also aim for an increased
nature-orientation at our sites and avoid additional soil sealing or land clearance. Our ambitions toward zero
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) deforestation and conversion in our value chain hold true for our own operation sites as well.
WATER AND MARINE The Sustainability Council regularly reviews the effectiveness of our policies and progress toward our objec-
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
tives, as well as the material impacts, risks and opportunities and their changes.
BIODIVERSITY AND Scope (E4-2_22, MDR-P_65b)
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
The provisions in this policy apply to the Henkel Group worldwide, including all operations and subsidiaries
as well as their upstream and downstream value chain. This includes sites managed in or near a biodiversity-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) sensitive area. No business units, regions, specific activities or stakeholders are excluded.

(ESRS S2) gic priorities, targets as well as risks and opportunities with respect to biodiversity and ecosystems. The Sus-

AFFECTED COMMUNITIES

(ESRS S3)
best practices, and the monitoring of the results. It also handles cross-departmental projects and continuously
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS reviews the extent to which biodiversity- and ecosystems targets are met. The business units (Adhesive
(ESRS S4) Technologies and Consumer Brands) and relevant corporate functions are responsible for the implementation
of sustainability policies, the planning of relevant actions as well as for the provision of necessary resources.
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)

Traceability of products, components and raw materials (E4-2_23d)
FOREWORD
Henkel strives to trace supplies upstream to the point where compliance can be ascertained using several
SCOPE AND REFERENCE approaches varying according to commodities: tracing material to the production unit (agricultural or
FRAMEWORK forestry land), or via a third-party certification scheme that is aligned with the company via supplier systems
that provide control back to the production unit level; or sourcing from areas documented to be low risk.
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
(ESRS 2) Social consequences of biodiversity and ecosystems-related impacts (E4-2_23f, AR 14, AR 15)
Human beings are strongly dependent on nature and biodiversity, e.g. in terms of natural pollination,
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
formation, protection and decontamination of soils and sediments and regulation of detrimental organisms.
Moreover, the natural world is an invaluable source of medicinal, biochemical, and genetic resources. Henkel
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
is committed to conducting its business in a manner that minimizes its environmental footprint and supports
WATER AND MARINE the preservation and enhancement of biodiversity to also support social sustainability and improve livelihood
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) of communities. Henkel also aims to contribute to the societal net-zero goal by mitigating emissions beyond
the own value chains (known as “beyond value chain mitigation”).
BIODIVERSITY AND
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) Sustainable land/agriculture practices or policies (E4-2_24b)
Henkel expects its suppliers to undertake best efforts to aim for sustainable production or sourcing of agri-
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
cultural or forestry feedstocks. This includes using agroforestry and other regenerative practices, wherever
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
possible.

Actions and resources related to biodiversity and ecosystems (E4-3)
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS Key current and planned actions (E4-3_27, MDR-A_68a, AR 22)
(ESRS S2) Our primary action in the area of biodiversity and ecosystems is the procurement of sustainable, certified raw
materials, particularly palm oil, palm kernel oil, and their derivatives, as well as paper, pulp and other wood-
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES based packaging products. This action supports achieving the targets that we set ourselves of “100 percent
(ESRS S3)
paper and cardboard materials recycled or sourced from sustainable forestry” and “100 percent palm oil or
palm kernel oil responsibly sourced, certified or externally verified.” This measure addresses material impacts
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
(ESRS S4) on land use changes and land degradation in the upstream value chain. Recognized certifications for palm
oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives are those provided by the Roundtable on Sustainable Palm Oil (RSPO),
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) either mass balance (MB) or segregated (SG). For wood-based packaging materials: Certifications from the
Forest Stewardship Council (FSC), Programme for the Endorsement of Forest Certification Schemes (PEFC),
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
Sustainable Forest Initiative (SFI), Canadian Standards Association (CSA), Australian Forestry Standard (AFS)
or equivalent certifications.

procurement of relevant raw materials, while also promoting social sustainability. Within the mitigation hier-
SCOPE AND REFERENCE archy, this relates to the aspect of avoidance. This action is also aimed at net-zero deforestation. For further
FRAMEWORK information, please refer to the section “Key content of the policy” (E4-2, MDR-P_65a) in this chapter. For
further information about the most important actions regarding the direct causes of biodiversity loss and

RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR Taking remedial actions (E4-3_27, MDR-A_68d)
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
The impacts that Henkel recognizes as material come into being in a complex way. Since the extent of Henkel’s
contribution to such impacts cannot usually be determined with certainty, the actions taken should not be
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
understood as remedial actions for those who have actually been harmed by material impacts. Rather, the
actions taken by Henkel represent a contribution to mitigating or avoiding impacts. Such actions, including
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) preventive measures, encompass, for example, technical and managerial instruments, including process
instructions, implementation of codes, standards and guidelines, as well as accompanying training.

Description of targets (E4-4_31)
(ESRS S2)
Henkel has set targets for the responsible sourcing of palm (kernel) oil and wood-based packaging materials,
as these are considered critical raw materials associated with a deforestation risk. Responsible sourcing includes
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
(ESRS S3) the use of certification systems that document deforestation-free procurement or responsible forestry
management. These targets address the identified material impacts of land-use change as a direct driver
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS of biodiversity loss and land degradation as an impact on biodiversity and ecosystems in the upstream
(ESRS S4)
value chain.
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)

The three targets are:
FOREWORD
 E4-T1: 100 percent palm and palm kernel oil responsibly sourced and certified or externally verified by 2025
SCOPE AND REFERENCE  E4-T2: 100 percent transparency and traceability for palm and palm kernel oil by 2025
FRAMEWORK  E4-T3: 100 percent paper and cardboard materials recycled or sourced from sustainable origins by 2025
GENERAL DISCLOSURES Relation between targets and policy objectives (E4-4_3, MDR-T_80a, AR 24, AR 25, AR 26)
(ESRS 2) These targets include the procurement of certified palm oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives, as well as
certified wood-based packaging materials. The certificates used confirm deforestation-free production or
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
responsible forestry management. In Henkel’s policies, sourcing raw materials aimed at achieving net-zero
deforestation is a key element in protecting biodiversity and ecosystems. This aligns with our nature policy.
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
Our goal is, in particular, to prevent the deforestation of primary and secondary forests with significant eco-
WATER AND MARINE logical value. These include peat lands and other high-carbon stock areas. Henkel is committed to achieving
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) the target of “net-zero deforestation” and expects its suppliers to support this objective under Henkel’s
Responsible Sourcing Policy.

ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) Performance against targets (E4-4_31, MDR-T_80j, AR 24, AR 25, AR 26)
E4-T1: The total amount of palm oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives purchased in 2024 was 79,002 metric
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR tons. Of this, 78,143 metric tons, or 99 percent, was used to determine target achievement. The proportion
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) of certified materials amounted to 97 percent. Target achievement is thus at 97 percent. The recognized
certifications are those provided by the Roundtable on Sustainable Palm Oil (RSPO), either mass balance (MB)
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
or segregated (SG). These certification systems include deforestation-free production as a criterion and
also address human rights and the inclusion of smallholder farmers. They therefore have significant social
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) impacts as well. The RSPO engages with indigenous peoples and advocates for their rights to prevent land
conflicts and associated human rights violations against them and other local communities. Progress toward
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES target achievement is determined on the basis of supplier order volumes, focusing on suppliers who deliver
(ESRS S3)
more than 25 metric tons of palm oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives to Henkel each year. A list of sup-
pliers within the scope is compiled quarterly in a palm oil report. Procurement data and the palm oil and
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
(ESRS S4) palm kernel oil content of purchased raw materials are used to calculate the absolute volume of palm oil
and palm kernel oil. An internal team checks the suppliers annually. Moreover, Henkel is externally certified
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) by SCS Global according to the RSPO Supply Chain Certification Standard. The RSPO status is published monthly
on the RSPO website.

E4-T2: In 2023, Henkel traced 95 percent of its purchased palm oil, palm kernel oil and derivatives back to
FOREWORD
the refinery, 94 percent to the oil mills and 65 percent to the plantation. The assessment is based on Henkel’s
SCOPE AND REFERENCE agreed baseline volume of 97.5 percent of the total procurement volume, which is extrapolated to the full
FRAMEWORK volume. Traceability to plantations, mills and refineries is evaluated to calculate the traceable share. The
traceability assessment is conducted by ASD (Action for Sustainable Derivatives), a collaborative initiative
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
that brings together companies from the cosmetics, home care, personal care and oleochemical sectors to
(ESRS 2)
jointly address environmental and social supply chain issues related to palm oil and palm kernel oil deriva-
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) tives. ASD has developed a method to map the complex supply chains of palm oil, palm kern oil and their
derivatives. The ASD process includes the steps “data collection from direct and indirect suppliers,” “supply
POLLUTION (ESRS E2) chain mapping,” “mapping of sourcing areas” and “assessment of the level of transparency.” Each year, data
from the supply chain is examined and collected through consultations with direct and indirect suppliers,
WATER AND MARINE
conducted by an independent third party, to identify sourcing areas and compile a list of refineries and mills
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
within the supply chain. Through ASD, Henkel works closely with supply chain stakeholders to clarify and
BIODIVERSITY AND align transparency requirements via webinars and one-on-one discussions. The values for the full year 2024
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4) will be published with the 2025 Sustainability Statement.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR E4-T3: In 2023, the total amount of wood-based packaging materials purchased was 222.4 metric kilotons,
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
with 64 percent comprising recycled materials and 34 percent certified non-recycled materials. Target
achievement is at 98 percent. Progress toward achieving the target is determined in collaboration with
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
suppliers and is based on their self-disclosures as part of the annual Pulp-Paper-Packaging (PPP) survey.
The recognized certifications are FSC, PEFC, SFI, CSA, AFS and/or equivalent certifications. The values for the
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
(ESRS S2) full year 2024 will be published in the Sustainability Statement 2025.

Identified ecological thresholds and method (E4-4_32a i)
FOREWORD
The target for palm oil and palm kernel oil pertains to tropical forests (ecological threshold: 85 percent of
SCOPE AND REFERENCE the tropical forest area compared to the potential tropical forest area during the Holocene). The target for
FRAMEWORK certified paper and cardboard applies to all three forest types, with ecological thresholds of 50 percent for
temperate and 85 percent for boreal and tropical forest area relative to the potential forest area during the
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
Holocene. The methodology for these thresholds is described in the Stockholm Resilience Centre’s planetary
(ESRS 2)
boundaries concept.
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
Entity-specific thresholds (E4-4_32a ii)
The threshold used is a global value and not entity-specific.

WATER AND MARINE

Three entity-specific metrics are also collected to measure progress toward the targets. These are:
FOREWORD
 Percentage of recycled and certified wood-based packaging materials purchased
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK  Percentage of certified palm oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives purchased
 Percentage of purchased palm oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives traceable to the refinery, mill or
GENERAL DISCLOSURES plantation
(ESRS 2)
The percentage of recycled and certified wood-based packaging materials purchased is determined in col-
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1) laboration with suppliers and based on their self-disclosures as part of the annual Pulp-Paper-Packaging
(PPP) survey. In this survey, suppliers report the total volumes of delivered material by certification system,
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
divided by recycled and virgin materials. The consolidated results provide the overall percentage of recy-
cled and certified materials through division by the total volume. The volumes covered by the survey
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3) amount to over 90 percent of the order volume of wood-based packaging materials; the values are linearly
extrapolated to 100 percent. The recognized certifications are FSC, PEFC, SFI, CSA, AFS and/or equivalent
BIODIVERSITY AND certifications. This metric is reported with a one-year delay, meaning it is included in this report for the year
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
2023. This delay is due to the supplier survey and subsequent data processing and verification, which take
place annually during the second and third quarters for the previous year.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
The percentage of certified palm oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives purchased is calculated by first
determining the actual quantity of palm oil and palm kernel oil used. This is done by multiplying the total
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
volume of purchased raw materials containing palm oil or palm kernel oil by their respective mass-based
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS content of palm oil or palm kernel oil. The amount of certified palm oil and palm kernel oil purchased is then
(ESRS S2) divided by this total quantity. The certified quantities are determined for all suppliers delivering more than
25 metric tons of material annually, which covers 99 percent of the palm oil and palm kernel oil-based raw
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES
material volumes. The calculated figure is extrapolated to 100 percent. The recognized certifications are those
(ESRS S3)
provided by the Roundtable on Sustainable Palm Oil (RSPO), either mass balance (MB) or segregated (SG).
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
(ESRS S4) The percentage of palm oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives traceable to the refinery, mill or plantation
is determined by ASD. The assessment is based on Henkel’s agreed baseline volume of 97.5 percent of the
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1) total procurement volume and is extrapolated to the full volume. To calculate the traceable percentage,
traceability to plantations, mills and refineries is assessed and then divided by the total volume of purchased
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION
palm oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives. The ASD process includes the steps “data collection from direct
and indirect suppliers,” “supply chain mapping,” “mapping of sourcing areas” and “assessment of the level of
transparency.” Each year, data from the supply chain is examined and collected through consultations with
direct and indirect suppliers, conducted by an independent third party, to identify sourcing areas and compile
a list of refineries and mills within the supply chain. This metric is reported with a one-year delay, meaning
HENKEL SUSTAINABILITY REPORT 2024 210
it is included in this report for the year 2023, as the analysis and verification of data are completed annually
FOREWORD
in the third quarter for the previous year.
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK External validation (MDR-M_77b)
Regarding the percentage of certified palm oil, palm kernel oil and their derivatives purchased, Henkel is certi-
GENERAL DISCLOSURES fied by SCS Global Services. The certification confirms compliance with the “RSPO Supply Chain Certification
(ESRS 2) Standard, version February 2020.” This guarantees that the criteria for processing RSPO-certified palm products
have been met through one or more supply chain models defined in the RSPO supply chain certification
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
systems. The metrics that are normally disclosed have been verified exclusively by our auditor as part of the
audit of this Sustainability Report with limited assurance.

Resource use and circular economy (ESRS E5)
FOREWORD
SCOPE AND REFERENCE
FRAMEWORK Strategy
Material impacts, risks and opportunities and their interaction with strategy and business model
GENERAL DISCLOSURES (ESRS 2, SBM-3_48b)
(ESRS 2)
Effective protection of natural resources requires decoupling economic growth from the consumption of
finite, natural and fossil resources and development to a circular economy. As part of Henkel’s materiality
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
assessment, negative and positive impacts on people and the environment, as well as potential risks and op-
portunities, were identified for our business regarding resource use and circular economy. For instance,
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)
inadequate waste management can pose risks to people and the environment. The use of non-renewable
WATER AND MARINE resources, such as fossil-based raw materials or packaging, can contribute to resource scarcity and increased
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)
greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. Neglecting circular economy principles in product design and production
processes may have negative effects on people and the environment. Conversely, positive impacts can be
BIODIVERSITY AND
expected when product formulations or packaging are based on secondary raw materials, are fully recyclable
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
and biodegradable. The growing demand for innovative products aligned with circularity principles offers
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR significant business opportunities for Henkel. However, potential financial risks arise for the Company if re-
ECONOMY (ESRS E5) placing fossil-based and non-circular ingredients with renewable materials is only feasible at higher costs.
Another cost factor could arise from new regulations requiring the establishment of packaging collection
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
and recycling systems.
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS
We view sustainability in our strategy as a competitive advantage, with the circular economy playing a key
(ESRS S2)
role. This includes a commitment to promoting the circular economy through our products, packaging and
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES technologies. Henkel aims to increase the share of ingredients based on renewable and secondary raw ma-
(ESRS S3) terials while reducing the use of fossil raw materials by reformulating products. For packaging, the proportion
of secondary raw materials will be further increased, while simultaneously reducing the overall amount of
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
packaging material used. All packaging should be designed for recycling or reusability. Henkel also aims to
(ESRS S4)
reduce waste from its own production sites, as well as from the upstream and downstream value chains.
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)
ADDITIONAL INFORMATION

Impact, risk and opportunity management
FOREWORD
Policies related to resource use and circular economy (E5-1)
SCOPE AND REFERENCE Key content of the policy (E5-1_14, MDR-P_65a, AR 10, E5-1_15a)
FRAMEWORK Henkel is working on the transition to a circular economy, aiming to align resource inflows and outflows with
circular principles.
GENERAL DISCLOSURES
(ESRS 2) Inflows
Our resource inflows are comprised of raw and packaging materials for our products. The Company’s
CLIMATE CHANGE (ESRS E1)
activities focus on the following areas:
POLLUTION (ESRS E2)  Increasing the proportion of raw materials based on renewable and recycled resources in product
formulations.
WATER AND MARINE
RESOURCES (ESRS E3)  Reducing the amount of raw materials used by reformulation of products.
 Increasing the proportion of recycled materials in packaging.
BIODIVERSITY AND  Reducing the amount of packaging materials to a minimum, without compromising the quality, performance
ECOSYSTEMS (ESRS E4)
or safety of products.
RESOURCE USE AND CIRCULAR Henkel strives to reduce the use of fossil-based materials to mitigate the environmental footprint and avoid
ECONOMY (ESRS E5)
contributing to the depletion of natural resources. Recognizing the Company’s reliance on material inflows,
Henkel is dedicated to support extended producer responsibility systems for packaging. Henkel is supporting
OWN WORKFORCE (ESRS S1)
and financing waste collection and recycling through Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR) fees. This sup-
VALUE CHAIN WORKERS ports that packaging waste at the end of its life cycle will be recycled and will then become available as
(ESRS S2) post-consumer recycled material to replace fossil-based virgin plastics.
AFFECTED COMMUNITIES Henkel expects all suppliers to minimize emissions by keeping materials within the economic cycle. Suppliers
(ESRS S3) are also expected to drive progress toward a circular economy by applying the 5Rs of the circular economy:
Reduce, Reuse, Repair, Recycle, Recover. Henkel strives to raise awareness about sustainable waste disposal
CONSUMERS AND END-USERS
in its own operations and among its suppliers.
(ESRS S4)
BUSINESS CONDUCT (ESRS G1)

ited to the OECD Due Diligence Guidance, the EU Regulation and the Dodd-Frank Act), our suppliers must
